THE DEPUTY TOWN-CLERKSHIP.

Report of the Sub-Committee.

A MEAGRE SALARY.

the following is the report of the sub-committee appointed to consider and report on the terms of appointment of Deputy Town Clerk. It is to be sub- mitted at the adjourned meeting of the Town Council next Tuesday evening :—Your sub-corn:nittee beg to re- port that they have considered the duties of the office of Deputy Town Clerk and incidently of each clerk in the Town Clerk's department. The duties have been defined by the Town Clerk,and the opportunity has been taken by him of arranging the duties of his staff con- sequent on Mr. Wilson's retirement. T.he duties of a purely clerical nature previously performed by Mr. Wilson have been transferred to other clerks, and it is proposed that the supervision of the staff should be transferred to Mr. Spinks' who has for some years filled the office of chief clerk. Your committee are of opinion that the person appointed deputy should be a qualified solicitor. The duties he will have to perform are as follows :—(1) To represent the Corporation in cases before justices and other tribunals of inferior jurisdiction. To assist the Town Clerk as from time to time required in the following matters: (2) Services required by the Mayor, chair- men, and deputy-chairmen of committees, in respect of their offices. (3) Parliamentary work. (4) Local inquiries by Government departments. (5) Arbitra- tions. (6) High Court of Justice actions and other litigation. (7) The conduct of Council and committee meetings, and advising thereat and preparation of retorts therefor. (9) The carrying out of orders of the Council and its committees. (9) To direct the Registration Clerk in the conduct of Parliamentary and naunicipal elections. (10) To render such other assist- ance as the Town Clerk may reasonably require in the performance of his duties. Your committee recommend that the salary to be offered be .2175, rising by annual increases of £l5 to a maximum of £250. While full consideration will no doubt be given to the qualifications of applicants who have had previous experience in a Town Clerk's office,it is not thought expedient to restrict the applications to those who have had such experience. Your committee are pleased to recommend that Mr. Thomas 11. Spinks be appointed the Assistant Town Clerk, and that he be entrusted with the duties which the Town Clerk has defined for him. Your committee also recommend that in recognition of the additional responsibility thrown upon Mr.Spinks his salary be increased from£l9o to£l4o per annum and that he have a further acrease 01.'10 next year. Your committee also recommend 1,11,,t Mr. Alfred Starkey be appointed the registration clerk, and that his salary be increased to £lOO per annum with a further increase of £lO next year.—William Bolton, D. Tinnion, John Pemberton, John Wilkinson, and William J. Forshaw.

THE STORY OF A LANCASHIRE GENIUS.

Success of a Warrington School of Art

Student.

—— " The art school system must be judged by results. Obviously one of the best results is in helping genius out of obscurity. Not that genius does not somehow as a rule help itself. It does ; but, all the same, a little help is worth a deal of sympathy. Happily, it is possible to point to aremarkable recent instance which has a local interest. A Wigan photographer had born to him 3m the eighties a son who, at the age of ten or less, xvith a nail for a chisel and a bolt tied on to a stick for a mallet, occupied himself in carving stones in a rude way. The lad at school was taught a little bit of elementary freehand drawing. He amused himself out of school hours in cutting out these patterns in relief on any stone surface that happened to be handy. Such an unusual turn drew no attention beyond amused curiosity, and at the age of eleven the boy began to earn his living by carrying out parcels for a grocer. But he attended, out of inclination, evening classes for drawing, and passed examinations with distinction. His former schoolmaster, hearing of this, told the father that the lad was certainly something of a prodigy, and prevailed upon him to send the boy to the Warrington School of Art. There the youth gained an exhibition worth £lO, and qualified for an art master's certificate. Hisv next step was to compete for one of the art scholarships offered by the Lancashire County Cduncil, this prize being worth £6O a year for three years. Here, however, a difficulty arose. The student was disqualified because his parants lived in a county borough, which should itself have founded scholarships out of the "whisky money," but had not thought it worth while. The parents cut the knot by making up their minds to move across the borough boundary, and although the father had a long tramp early and late to the pit in which be earned his daily bread, his son succeeded in winning the coveted dis- tinction. With it he went at the age of nineteen, to Paris, entered the studio of a famous sculptor, gained five medals for drawing, sent his original work to the Salon, won the grande medaille, and was placed hors conconrs—given, that is to say the right to exhibit in future on the ground of proved merit—and was elected a scholar of the Ecole des Beaux Arts both as sculptor and painter. In view of this brilliant fulfilment the Lancashire County Council madehis scholarship tenable for an additional year. Works by John Millard, showing the unmistable touch of that magnificent school of modern sculptor of which Paris is the centre, as well as the force and quality of innate originality, have been seen in the Autumn Exhibition in Liverpool, and two examples are in the exhibition 'now open at Sonthport. The fact that there is so uncommon a story behind these exhibits is calculated to arrest attention, but there is a story, did we but know it, behind all art work that rises above the beaten level.

Art has its tragedies as well as its fulfilment. Un- fortunately the artistic consciousness of tho public lags ' behind art teaching. But the time may come when the public will discover that one tithe of the sums now squandered on transient luxuries, which yield but small satisfaction in proportion to their cost, would, if spent with liberal discernment on the encouragement of art, add lastingly to the joy of living. Especially is this the case with the neglected art of sculpture, for al- though the revived modern sculpture is, in its best masterpieces, equal in power and beauty to the work of Ancient Greece as we know it, our cities generally lack the cheerful adornment which art can afford, and our houses the noble air of hospitality and repose which a taste for sculpture gives to so many of the chateaux of the wealthy in Prance. Assuredly, however, it is just as profitable to spend money on artists as upon milliners and cooks.—" Liverpool Daily Post and Mercury."

THE MAUCTVZSA VITELVESCHL

The Marchesa Vitelleschi, whose new book, "The Romance of Savoy," has attracted so much atten- tion, is one of the three gifted sisters of Lord Lamington, now Governor of Bombay. These popular beauties of the Cochrane-Baillie family all made interesting unions, the eldest marrying, in 1.867, the Earl de ta Warr, who died nine years ago, and again taking-a husband, three years hack, in the person of the Rev. Paul Wyatt, Chaplain of the famous Chapel Royal in the Savoy for the last ten years. The youngest of the sisters married Viscount Melville twelve years ago, and their favourite home—for they have more than one —is Melville Castle, in Midlothian. The gifted lady who found her life partner in Italy, and went. to Rome to live, has long been immersed in the hi-story and Amance of that interesting part of Southern Europe, and 'knows what she is writing about when she takes up the story of Savoy

Swint SESII-EVENING BLOUSE.

Two girls were waiting outside a theatre, In the well-dressed feminine queue that is to be found near many London theatre doors on a fine Wed- nesday afternoon. They were discussing the probability of the genial weather lasting over the Easter holidays. "Where are you going this year?" asked the taller girl. "Somewhere abroad. We haven't decided yet," was her companion's answer. Then followed a discussion on the various items of dress needed for different places mentioned. The me who overheard this conversation drew the con- clusion that smart blouses for table d'lstite wear

seemed the'biggest consineration, SO for the benefit of similarly puzzled girls our present illustration chews a dainty semi-evening blouse which would well serve ,on such occasions. It is fashioned in the seftest•of oyster-tinted satin, and has a cross- over pouf which almost entirely covers the bodice. From under the outer edge there peeps a graduated frill of lace, and the smart little sleeves are frilled with the same filmy lace, while the low neck is filled in with folds of spotted chiffon. The high draped belt is finished with a pretty arrange- ment of bows in front, which completes this sug- gestion for a semi-evening bodice.

FASHION NOTES.

The shops are shewing garments for summer days, and already, writes "Amica," one sees whither fashions tend. Silks in light shades will be much worn; full skirts will continue popular; while many of the thin materials, such as voiles, will be printed :In colours. Small checks are worn, and shepherds' plaids, with white vests and black cloth coats, are popular. Hats are much beflowered, and a bid for favour is made by a hat that is plate-shaped, worn well over the forehead, and tilted high behind, with a garden of small flowers to rest on the hair at the back. This is not at all a becoming shape to average mortals; young and very pretty people can wear anything. Small hats are an innovation, but the large hats are too generally becoming to be aban• doned easily. Parasols are smaller than they were, and the folding parasol stick, which was the thing half a century ago, has reappeared. Short skirts for morning wear will soon be universal, but for the afternoon and evening the sweeping skirt will long bold its own.

===== 0002601_19050408_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
Loos E COAT.

Our new coats must be either of the trimmest and dressiest description, or must be very long and loose; and while silk is much used for the former style, white and cream drap souple appears to be the material for the latter variety. The sketch at present under our notice is a type of the long, loose coat in a deep cream tint. The double pull sleeves end in a cuff of deep amethyst velvet, overlaid with silver and gold braid of the narrowest kind, and finished with big shaped frills, the collar being shrlilarly treated. The huge buttons down each side may be connected by thick silk cord loops. We are told that the closer-fitting hats and toques that our French sisters have adopted are

not, as a rule, becoming to Englishwomen. Thls. statement should be taken cautiously. The present writer spent some time in a West-End milliner's salon yesterday, and while there watched many of these new hats tried on- by women with widely differing appearances, and in most cases, where the hair was well arranged, the eltapeavx proved most becoming. A very good example of the i "pill-box " toque is shewn n the present sketch. It is entirely composed of small shaded flowers, with a smart cockade of soft satin ribbon by way of trimming.

TO-DAT°S RECIPE.

SALMON CUTLETS.—Take a small picre of cooked salmon, pound it well in a mortar, add to it an equal weight of line breadcrumbs, and half the weight in butter: mix well together, and season with pepper, salt, cayenne; bind the mixture with the beaten yolks of two' eggs, roll it Out on a floured board, cut it with a cutter into cutlets, roll them in egg and breadcrumbs; repeat this, so that they are well covered. Fry in boiling fat, drain; servo very hot, with fried parsley in the centre of the dish. Serve maitre d'hôtel sauce will)

this dish.

A WONDERFUL SCENE AT SEA,

FliOM Tilt NOTES OF REPEUT DE LA TOUCUE

in: W. CLARK RUSSELL (Wiakor jf " The Wreck of the Grosvenor," " Overdue," 4.e.).

The following is an account set down by the second mate of one of the ships concerned in that much-debated convict-carrying trade whidn is, now receding into the far past of Empire's annals. It has comp into my bands by a strauza chance of fortune, and is, substantially, given in its original form. - " We win a not less memorable and histtecal flea than that which sailed in charge of the renowned Columbus ; but the nation was mind- ing its own business and did not heed us. So little did it attend to us that as we slowly got our anchors by the clumsy labour of the mer- chant-men, and as the yards that spread our sails leisurely mounted to their mast-heads I doubt if there was an eye on the beach or either hand of us that took notice of our departure, unless it were some lurking; .idling waterman who, sprawling with arms akimbo upon a post and chewing his black junk of tobacco, says to his companion: "Them convict ships is -off at last, Bill," and that might be all. Nevertheless we, were the ships destined by Providence to expand the empire of -out country by establishing a colony in Australia, or New Holland, as that spacious island-cern-1 tinent was formerly termed. We yere eleven ships in all—a sorrowful, melancholy, pitiful, ill-equipped, lamenting procession. We were five transports or convict ships laden with male or female felons ; and my ship, I may say here, was the " Lady Penrhyn " of three hundred and, thirty tons, manned by thirty sailors and our freight wai3 a hundred and two women. Other ships were ' the " Sirius " of twenty guns, flying the broad pennant of a Commodore, and some victualling vessels, together -with a wet and surly little craft called the "Supply," an armed tender of one hundred and seventy tons, eight guns and fifty men. These ships were to enlarge our empire by Embracing a new con- tinent which even in this year of my writing (1825) promises to rival the United States of America in the beauty and richness of its towns end cities, in the magnificence of its harbours, in the excellence and extent of its ports and the multiitude of shipping that visits them,. in the value and variety of its natural products and exports, outrivalling the opulence of Chili and Peru when those nations of the silver god were whitening the ocean with the . sails of pirate. haunted galleons. " When we -were some degrees north of the equator we fell in with such a strange and amaz- ing scene of nature that I am constrained to describe it. I never eau doubt but that the con- victs who were on deck at the time were con- vinced that the Captain had betrayed, the pro- mises of Commodore P—, who had . talked •t. them of a 'bright and flowery country, where, it they conducted themselves with propriety, they would end in becoming the mistresses of houses and land—by sailing them into a part of the uni- verse which was unknown to man and perhaps not a part of this globe, full of thunders and fires and terrors, of Pagan rites and bloody sac- rifices.

"The hour was shortly before eleven in the forenoon. All the females were on deck taking the exercise that was part of their discipline. It was extremely warm. I scar,..ely remember a hotter day. The pitch in the seams was as soft as butter and stuck to the feet, and if you touched the woodwork exposed to the • sun you raised a blister. The sea rolled softly and breathlessly. It was of the colour of brass, shining with a kind of polished mistiness under the sim which flamed with a troubled face. The walls of the sky were hung with mist amid which you dimly discerned the fignres of the distant ships of the fleet looming large and rolling sulkily, and the bowsprit of every ship, uncontrolled upon that swelling burnished surface, which looked as yellow as a guinea, pointed hi a different direc- tion.

"On a sudden it seemed to grow gloomy, as though a cloud had passed over the sun, though when I looked up I did not observe that his foggy brightness was diminished. At the same moment a tremendous commotion happened round about us, and away. For the distance of a league or tii o from the ship's sides, the water was set a-boiling, and such volumes of steam arose that you could not see the sky for them. A great flame shot out of the -xater not above a mile distant from where we lay helpless. This volcanic outburst was attended by a roar- ing noise as of shocks of thunder one after an- other. Some of the females were so afraid that they fell upon their knees, and here and there you could see a woman crossing herself. "Whilst the water still boiled a huge black whale was thrown up dead; it rose within a musket shot of us. When some of the less timid of the females beheld this tremendous appari- tion, never having seen a whale before, nor. per- haps heard of one. unless on a Sunday from the lips of theprison chaplain when he read about Jonah, whom they might have considered a hop-o'-my- thumb rather than a man to be swallowed up by and live inside of any sort of fish, they set up a screech that it was a rock, that the ship would strike upon it and sink. No one heeded them. The uproar and surprising upheaval without raised too many alarms within to suffer the cries of a few ignorant women to engage attention. " Presently the steam passed and the water ceased to boil; the colour of the ocean changed into a blood red, and a • horrid smell arose from it; the surface was covered with dead fishes: fish of all kinds and sizes ; great fishes on their backs, with their bellies gleaming white in the blood-red setting of water; strange shaped fishes on. their sides, painted as gaily as the flowers of a hot-house nosegay; long black fishes moving serpent-wise in their deadness on the swell. "The seamen got into the channels and with buckets and other contrivances drew up enough fish, some of which were already partially boiled, to serve us at both ends of the ship for half a score of meals. And let me tell you that there is not in this wide world a sweeter dish than a liberal fry of fresh fish at sea after you have • been used for many weeks to the stone- hard beef, and flint-hard biscuit on which we mariners are fed. I heartily wished that fish for the poor women had been drawn up. Had not God in this smiting of His ocean and the creatures inhabiting it, provided plenty ? Might not the haul, without irreverence, have been likened to the miraculous draught ? But the women were convicts and not to be pampered, so they went without whilst the sailors ate; though God knows ne'er a man aft or forward but would have shared his meal with( the women. For no matter in what opinion Jack may be held, he has a heart for compassion ; and if there is one man more willing than another to help a poor suffering woman, he is a sailor. "The horrible smell of the sea continued, and combining with the heat rendered the atmo- sphere scarce endurable. Some of the women gave way and swooned in earnest, so the Captain ordered all of them below. _

"The ocean remained a ghastly red until the af- ternoon, and we all pined for a breeze to blow us away clear of the smells and discoloured waters coated with dead fish, huge amongst which, and close to our ship, towered the dead leviathan of the deep, a grey and hideous object that darted wet gleams as it lightly lurched with the swell ; a menace that, unless wind came, its corruption would convert, this spot of ocean into a climate thick and noxious with the viewless fever, and the deadly unseen pestilences that stalk out of decay. " About four o'clock, how ever, the sea recovered its natural colour by deposition, I suppose, of the scarlet matter which had been upheaved into the volume of the water—as smoke spreads ant US the body of the atmosphere—by the tremendous shock at the bottom of the sea ; and shortly after our eyes were gladdened by a sight of the burnished calm in the north-east turr:mg• a ripener Puri purer tint to the busy brushing of the breeze. One by one the ships took this arc of -v4nd and swmig to it, every ship troubling th 4, loiter into foam under her bows, with the sails acrelling and glistening in the afternoon light as though wrought of silk; and thus, over the iiiow blue ocean, frolicsome with ripples, every' ripple alining with its curl of froth that san4 salt and cool in the wind, did 'the ships of the fleet, coming together, sail on; away from that circle poisoned by Volcanic spasm, and in an hour or two we bad lost sight of the rock-like loom of the dead whale. " This I have made a note of as an incident of Cormnodore P—'s voyage recorded. in. two - only of the log-books of the ships, but 'to imperfectly as to convey no idea of the wonderful scene of that submarine earthquake, and the fulness of its hoxror:l, in lightnings; thunders, vapours and

Saturday,

[ALL• RIGHTS RESERVED.]

April 8,

BACHELOR BROTHERS,

RACHEL CHALLICE (Author of " Vexed Questions," 4.c.).

I i this age of enquiry it seems curious that so little attention is paid to an obvious leakage from the store of our nation's strength--t mean the gradual decrease of available Bachelor Brothers, and ;the consequent loss to the ever- pmcpt Spinster Sisters. Pennle flock to hear Mr. Chamberlain on the Fiscal- Question, the, speeches are read with the deepest interest, the matter is freely discussed, and it is to be hoped that some system for the improvement of our trade may be established. But the social 'difficulty of,the overstock of men in our 'colonies and the superabundance of women in England is only considered by a few would-be reformers. Mr. Chamberlain certainly spoke forcefully on the subject at the meeting of the British Women's Emigration Association at the Imperial Institute last year. Nevertheless - few steps are taken to -make emigration attractive to gentlewomen, and after all, it is more a Question, for families to arrange than for Societies to take in hand. As a barrister from New Zealand said, when speaking on the matter at the Women's Insti- tute : "We do not want the riff-raff of Eng- lish women sent to us. we want women of high character, lit to •be the Mothers of our sons." To leave our colonies without such women whilst they idle by the English hearth is poor economy. Men sent adrift with no girls of their own set at hand, often take 'to themselves wives of the " cow-boy" class if they are in the Far West, or of the barmaid type in other places. " It is not good for man to be alone," and what is he to do, if the hands of those who would have loved and tended him hang uselessly by their side at home, or toy with some fancy needlework to be sold for the baptism of black babies ?

THE ENGLISH MOTHER. AND TIIE COW-BOY " GIILL.

The seriousness of this state of affairs be- COMPS almost comic in the mouth of a lady from Manitoba who related her, attempts to save her son and his friend from marrying girls •of the " cow-boy " class : " But what was Ito do ?" she said, pathetically ; " there were no nice English girls, for them to consort with, and so my task was an impossible one. Unfortunately I had to come back to England for a few weeks ; and I saw, by the shame-faced countenances of the young men on my return, that something had happened. What is it,' I asked my son's friend ; you don't mean to say you are en- gaged to cow-boy girl '?' His silence was sufficient answer. And you, too?' I asked my son, almost fiercely. ' Yes; me too !' was the reply, regardless of grammar ; and now I try to forget that my baby grandson is con- nected with a class whose constant presence makes my son's home impossible for me." If parents gave their daughters the S/IMO com- petence as their sons, they would not be a burthen on their brothers in the event of co- emigration. The payment of their share of expenses would give the young men a pleasant some of " camaraderie," and would not the the temporary , partnership in a new world be pleasant to both ? "I suppose when you grow up I shall have to keep you ?" I once heard an intelligent public school boy say to his sister, a bright little girl of ten. " Indeed you won't ?" retorted the child, with a toss of her curls ; "I shall be able to keep myself." And the prophecy was true, for, thanks to the father giving the girl a fair educa- tion, she subsequently gained a good post as a professor of music, and made -a very happy marriage. The five other sisters were also fairly launched in life, and now have comfortable homes of their own.

GIVE YOUR DAUGHTER A CHANCE.

But fathers, as a rule, offer a " passive rer sistance " to aiding their daughters to a career of their own. The home is the one and only resource suggested, whilst the tax of a fair dis- bursement for an equipment fitting them for independence is ignored. A man I know is now quite impoverished by his sons, who one after the other closed- their college careers by marry- ing a low-clasa girl. These second-rate house- holds are supported by the ageing parent, and the sisters at home find themselves poorer and poorer as each year lessens the chance of marry- ing or supporting themselves. Would not a quarter of the money spent upon the sons have helped the daughters to help themselves? I rather think I should like to marry," said a young man I know, who has a Govern- ment appointment on Lake Tanganyka ; "but even if anybody does turn up in the neighbour- hood, a fellow can't ask her to his bungalow without a lady to entertain her. An English- woman did once come, on her way across Africa alone, but the niggers found her their match, so you can be sure she was rattler too much for a white man. It's awfully lonely out there," he added; "and it would be nice for fellows abroad to feel they have one or two pleasant homes to go to in the evening. You see, we men. when quite alone, get rather like savages, and only meet to have a feed or to play cards." • Lake Tangavyka would not commend itself to every girl, although, as a matter of fact, I learnt afterwards that my young friend and his " mate " decided to take their sisters out to Africa at th end of their respective furloughs, and one does not need to be a Miss Braddon or a Rhoda Broughton to surmise the consequence of the establishment of the two brother-and- sister menages. So if the germ of two romances was thus sown in Central Africa, how many more might be started in the more congenial lands of Canada, Australia, New Zealand, &c. Many people in our land now complain of the importation of goods without any tax payment, but may not our colonies object to the large influx of men dumped down into their midst without protec- tion—the protection of a good woman's in. fiuence ?

COLONIAL PROTECTION,

----- Well would it be -if they said : "We decline to have this quantity of men without • the necessary company of a wife or a sister to see that he has the comforts common to an English labourer at home. Men thus forlorn and cheer- less flood our hotel bars and gambling places, and soon grow unfit to be good colonists." . And what do the sisters themselves say, for it is also as their advocate that I speak? Every student of character knows the pride a young girl takes in her bachelor brothers. And, un- knowng to themselves, the first heart-beats of their young companions are for the idealised individuals of their school-fellow's relations. And how is this early dream realised ? The girls leave school to return to their different homes. Their respective mothers call upon each other; they assemble on their "At Horns" days, to drink tea and chat. But where are "Torn." " Dick." and "Harry," the lads whose qualities' stirred the admiration of the girls? They are only present in their photo- graphs, placed conspicuously on the piano or tables, for as they are clerks, doctors, barristers, Sm., they are kept at the wheel all day, and thus their acquaintance with the girls described so attractively by their sisters is nil, for the drawing-room is deserted when they return borne in the evening. Is not this social system very like that of the old-fashioned country barometer, which never permits the little man and woman—represent- ing respectively good and bad weather—to be out or in at one and the same time? and is it not curious that this system of contrary motion should be practised when propinquity is one of the great means of compassing happy, and therefore desirable, marriages? Well-dressed, attractive girls flood the drawing-rooms in the afternoon, and in the evening, when Tom, Dick or Harry could look in, they have left, and so the young men retire to music balls and hotel billiard rooms, to seek the society which they ' should find at home; and who knows whltt ittesalliancts robzlit be prevented if drawing- reerns were there 'weft us havens of Isocull re- , creation in the evening

It seem strange that mothers anxious for the mell-being of their daughters should be blind to the disadvantage of the separation of their girls from young men. The girls can- not plead' for 7thoili,elv,,, so the desert of their lives grows more and more arid, the eases of pleasant companionship, becolle wirer, and the vulture of canal picks the flesh from their bones. Would tot this oiniti fly for hence if pleasaut companionship without the traditional, warpailit suggested , 'Woks to read, Music to study, and sundry other resources for which sympathy double the interest. " lint what nonsense. !"• I hear some matrow: exclaim, ". we gave three brilliant. At Homes ' last season :"

WORK FOR LOVE OR WORK FOR DAY.

True, bit is it not a mockery to use the word " At. Home " to describe to gathering in which one iS anything but at home ! For can one feel at ease with only rout seats to sit on ? with re- freshments struggled for in, a seething crush ? and with an entertainment of the character of a concert hall or theatre? The daughters of the house are lost in the crowd, and " Tom," " Dick," and " Harry" wonder why they troubled to struggle with studs, stiff shirts, and dress clothes, to be bandied about like hotel waiters ! Then the time comes when the girls hear that the same " Tom," " Dick," or " Harry," are going abroad—sheep-farming, to a Government post. as a barrister or doctor in Australia or New Zealand, or the Far West—and the men fade away like the unknown ,quantity of an un- prospected land. `• But," say our bachelor brothers, " how , could we expose our sisters to the hardships of a colonial life ?" "Are such hardships," I reply, " harder than those often suffered in England ?' Work, as we know, is the least of the hard-. ships of life. The greatest call made upon sympathy is the weariness of an unwanted life. The natural fatigue of healthy effort is a feather's weight in comparison. Moreover, ~the spinster sisters left at home by the submerged bachelor brothers are frequently exposed to efforts and fatigues worse than those of a colonial life. Is ten hours' work at a type- writer, in an overheated, overcrowded office, leasjer than a day spent in cooking, cleaning, !o poultry tending in the wholesome atmos- phere of a colonial farm, would be with a brother, moreover, to share the burden of life F or is the mechanical work of copying letters more intellectual than bringing the brain to bear upon the interesting questions of farm produce or the concoction of wholesome dishes? " Wonfen can always find work in England," I hear some objectors say. Yes ; posts can occasionally be found in infirmaries and hospitals. but, as we know, the young are always preferred at such centres, and where is the provision for a loveless old age ? "But how about the tax of emigrating a daughter with a son ?" I hear it asked, and I reply: "Is not such a tax a righteous tax, both for the sake of the spinster sisters and the bachelor brothers—for the former, expatriated to a homeless life in the real sense of the word, in the colonies, and for the d ttei, exposed to an aimless existence at home P Would not the effort of meeting the expense be, a noble policy, ceinenting family relation and freeing the home of idie and useless girls ? Emigration on such lines would be shorn of half .its difficulties, for is not the companionship of a brother better than the chaperonage of a stranger ? and would .not a step favoured by the sympathetic help of the family be taken with greater gusto than unclet the auspices of a Society ?"

MOTOR NOTES.

Although the Motor Car Act of 1903 has now been in force for over fifteen months, it is quite seldom that one encounters an amateur motorist who has a really clear comprehension of its various sections. This is, perhaps not very astonishing. What is sprprising is that b 0 many motoring journalists should not only have a misconception of the Act themselves, but should be so determinea to impress their mistaken notions upon others.

As an example of this we may quote a statement appearing in the motor column of one of our comtem- poraries. Says the writer : "If you have a car on trial for a day or two —or even for a week or two—there is little doubt that you can drive it where you please with the maker's plate, provided you wear it properly. But suppose you get your car on trial for a month, or three months,or twelve months? Would the police prosecute von? And if they did, would the court convict you? I am not quite so sure that they would not."

As the gentlemen appears to be,by his own confession in considerable doubt concerning the position he con- jures up, we have pleasure in explaining it to him in all its bearings. A manufacturer or an agent is only. allowed to take out upon his trial car an intending purchaser, or to test the car itself " after completion." He may not use it for running about on ordinary business, nor may he take his own pleasure trips upon it. Indeed, last year one manufacturer was summonqi—and subsequently lined for driving on his trial car, (Which bore the manu- facturers' mark assigned him), although he. was even then upon his way to pick up an intending purchaser for a trial. The act says clearly that a car so labelled can only be used for an intending purchaser, it says nothing about fetching him or taking him home, and, although so strict a reading of the section is ridiculous, it holds good.

Of course, the real trouble is that the Act was a piece of panic legislation rushed hurriedly through. Conse- quently, it is most loosely and carelessly strung together, many of its provisions as they stand being either foolish or quite unreasonable.

Our own experience leads us to believe that it is the matter of heavy repair bills whichl discourages the , budding motorist. He is prepared for the more or less ' heavy outlay, and he meets it like a man. He has also reckoned up the usual running expenses and allowed for them with generosity. But it is these occasional , bills of £l.O or £2O which stagger him. His chauffeur —through whose fauls it is more than probable that I most of the mechanical troubles have really risen—leads ; him to believe that the experience is quite usual. He insinuates that a back axle may he as easily bent as a thin lubricating pipe; but prudently says nothing of the side-slip, when driving thirty miles an hour over the ' grease, that occasioned it. In the same manner he conveys that every mishap, which is really due to his , own bad driving, is only what may be expected, and so the owner becomes dissatisfied with his new and expen- give pastime, sells the car at is sacrifice, discharges the chauffeur—and returns with added zest to ping-pong. The distressing fatal accidents which have recently occurred to two little children, both of whom, when playing in the road, wore knocked down and killed by ; motor cars, should impress upon parents the urgent necessity of keeping their little ones within bounds. It may be argued that the road is the only playground for :the children of thepoor; but Welwyn Dip—to quote one place where a fatality occurred—cannot seriously be regarded as a suitable one. The road here is narrow and very steep, dipping down as its name indicates straight into Welwyn.and with so many better places at hand all children should be warned against playing hero as danger is not only to be apprehended from cars, but from cycles and horse drawn vehicles as well.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

sisk:GESTIBW

I The Grand Old Lady of the—Peerage—The late ! Dowager Duchess of Abercorn was one of the most re- markable women Society has ever known, and her death ! in her ninety-third Year has thrown a great many noble families into mourning. Some few months ago the I venerable duchess had a serious illness, and was hardly expected to recover, but her wonderful vitality stood ' her in good stead,and, until a recent date, her Grace was active, and took a keen interest in all her family, and social affairs. No one would have supposed from her , portrait, which was in last year's Academy, that she I had so greatly exceeded in age, the Psalmists allotted ! span, and it is only a short time ago that she was at a I wedding in company with the Baroness Burdett-Coutts, ' who is now within a week or two of herninety-first birth- day. She made her first acquaintance with Court life in ! 1824, when George IV. was King, and was from first to last a great friend of our late Queen. The Duchess was one of the beauties of the Victorian era, but though filling a prominent place in the social world, she had strong religious feelings, which dominated the course of her long and remarkable life. Her marriage with the first Duke of Abercorn took place in 1832, when she was twenty, and her married life lasted for 53 years, the Duchess having been a widow since 1885. Society in Mourning.—The Duchess was the mother of 14 children, and her direct descendants numbered 245, of whom 1,66 survive. They constitute in themselves a miniature Burke's Peerage," as nearly all the children and grandchildren of the late Duchess have inter-married into noble families. Five of her seven sons survive and also five of her seven daughters. These are the Dowager Countess of Lichfield, the Duchess of Buccleuch, the Countess of Winterton, the Marchioness of Blandford and the Marchioness of Lansdowne. It has been said that the death of the Dowager Duchess will throw half the Peerage into mourning, but though this is going beyond the mark, the sad event will have rather a serious effect upon the London season. Many members of her family, it is true' confine their entertaining to the country, but Lady Lansdowne is one of the leading hostesses in Society. The Duke of Marlborough was contemplating giving some large entertainments in his new town house this' season, as was also the case with the Duchess of Leeds, who has young daughters to bring out, and these, as well as many other social fixtures, will be affected by the death of the venerable Duchess. The Duchess's Narrow Escape.—The late Dowager Duchess of Abercorn was in the terrible railway accident which happened to the Irish mail at Abergele on the 20th August, 1868, and no doubt owed her escape from death to an alteration in the "make up" of the train at Chester. Her husband, the first Duke of Abercorn was Lord Lieutenant at that time, and the Duchess, together with several members of her family, including the pre- sent Lady Lansdowne, were on their way to join him at the Viceregal Lodge in Dublin. They were travelling in the carriage next to the engine, when by some chance circumstance three carriages were added at Chester,and placed in the front part of the train. Shortly after- MR. CHAMBERLAIN. wards there was a collision with some rietroleum trucks, and the three first carriages were instantly enveloped in flames. Their thirty-three occupants, including Lord and Lady Farnham, were burnt to death, but all the To Speak at St. Helens Early in June. remaining passengers including the Hamilton! party escaped. The Bute Wedding.—There is some disappointment in Catholic circles that the marriage of Lord Bute and Miss Bellingham is not to take place in London. Lord Bute is one of the wealthiest peers, but the splendour of a great Society wedding has no attraction for him, and it is in accordance with his wish tkat the marriage is to be celebrated in the little town of Castle Belling- ham, in County Louth. It is to take place in June, the two principal bridesmaids being Lady Margaret Stuart, Lord flutes only sister, and Miss May Bellingham. The future bride is a daughter of Sir Henry Bellingham, who comes from a very old North of England family, and it was at his second wedding with MiSsßellingham's stepmother, that Lord Bute first made the acquaintance of his fiance. He is a great sportsman, and has only recently returned from a big game shooting expedition in Africa. Up-to-date Jewellery.—The number of Society weddings arranged to take place in London during Easter week, is almost unprecedented, the reason being that only six days are available between Lent, and the proverbially unlucky month of May. Tailors, dress- makers, and milliners are working at nigh pressure, and the wedding gift problem is one'likely to weigh heavily in many quarters. Jewellery is still a very favourite wedding present, though the motor, and household furniture, have run it close,during the past two or three seasons. Jewellers are more than busy, devising new shapes and combinations, the latest tiaras being so built up that it is possible to tip them with contrasting gems For example a diamond ornament, recently ordered for a bride elect, is so arranged that it may form a tiara or necklace, as desired, a set each of emeralds, turquoises and sapphires, accompanying it, and with which it may be tipped at will. This tiara and necklace, moreover, takes to pieces, and forms corsage ornaments. A com- bination of relatives is offering this handsome present,a sensible notion which is steadily gaining ground as time goes on. Freaks in Gems.—Few people seem to be aware that there are black diamonds, which flash and sparkle just as much as white ones; that red diamonds exist, also blue, canary coloured, and grass green diamonds. Need- less to say these freaks are few and far between, and fabulous prices are demanded and given for them. With the so-called black pearl we are more familiar, but few among us have seen a mauve, or even a pink pearl. Such rarities are generally caught up by American heiresses, to whom a few thousands more or less is of little moment. Becoming Veils.—Contrary to expectation the veil is remaining fashionable, worn slightly full beneath the chin, and gathered neatly up behind. The new flesh coloured tulles, which are alrnosc invisible, and bear chenille and velvet spots of all sizes, ranging from that of a threepenny piece to one of a pin head, are the most becoming we have seen for a considerable time, and the best selection is the medium-sized velvet spot, placed at mach rare intervals that only two or at most three, are noticeable. The spots play the same part as the old world patches, and greatly enhance the beauty of the complexion and eyes. Straight Hair Worn.—A year ago it was impossible to wave our tresses too niuch:the more ondittle we could get into them, the more fashionable we were, but we have changed all that, and at the moment perfectly straight hair is modish. Only the very few to whom Nature has been generous as regar is quantity, a beauti- ful colour and sheen, would however do well to follow this idea. The generality of coiffures are greatly improved by the use ot the waving irons, but the woman who would be in Fashions's Van must remember to restrict themselves most carefully to moderation, in this direction, and by no means to puff out the hair at the sides. Fashionable Needlework.—ln the way of needlework, the barbaric and gorgeous are preferred just now, and Maori work stands high in favour. This consists of odd native designs—the form of a grotesque god being the usual centrepiece, with green scroll-like surroundings— stitched on linen canvas, and outlined in crewel stem stitch in black filoselle. Wandering touches of black, and white filoselle intermingle with the scroll design which at regular and frequent intervals shows squares of bright hued silk worked over to form other squares, with black filoselle. This work is usually made up in the form of cushions, mantel drapery, fender stools, brackets, small table covers, etc. and is most effective. Moreover it is quickiy done, and by no means expensive. Sensitive Skins.—The woman who is troubled with a sensitive skin should use for the face, and in her bath, instead of soap, a small bag made of butter muslin, and filled with some of the following mixture :—Two pounds of fine oatmeal, one quart of clean bran, three quarters of a pound each of almond meal and powdered orris root, half-a-pound of white Castile soap, and an ounce and a half of primrose sachet. This should be kept in a closely stoppered bottle, the bags being filled when required for use. MAD GE.

Leo Pilkington. chairman of the Con- servative party, informed a St. Helens Press representa- tive on Tuesday that it had been decided that the great Lancashire demonstration, on the occasion of a visit from Mr. Joseph Chamberlain, is to be held at St. Helens early in June. The occasion will be the annual meeting of the Lancashire and Cheshire Consery ,ti ye Workingmen's Federation, and Sir Henry Seton-Karr, M.P., for St. Helens, being chairman of the Federation, it was generally felt that his constituency had claims which could not be overlooked.

It is stated that the gathering will take place in the extensive tram-shed of the St. Helens and District Tramway Company. It is estimated that seating accom- modation will be provided for over 8.000 persons. A temporary wooden flooring will be put down, and qpecial seating accommodation provided. PvtiNCORS FLAT FATALITY.

William Joseph Harrison, fifty-two, captain of the flat Herbert, was heaving at the winch preparatory to leaving Preesall for Liverpool when tho chain broke, and a heavy block struck him on the head, rendering him unconscious. He was removed to Fleetwood Cottage Hospital, where he died.

WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF ' COMMERCE.

The Lay-by at Walton. --- It was announced at a meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce which was held on Monday that the matter of providing a lay-by at Walton was engaging the serious attention of the directors of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. The Chamber also spent some considerable time discussing the question of the nation- alization of canals. Ultimately the secretary was instructed to convene a general meeting to consider the question and to ask Mr. F. W. Monks to introduce the subject. Alderman BENNETT presented the report of the dele- gates deputed to attend the annual meeting of the Associated Chamber of Commerce. The most important question considered he said was the extension of our canal system, and after a long discussion the following resolution was carried :—" That the association recom- mends that the improvement and extension of the canal system of the United Kingdom shall be carried out by means of a public trust, and, if necessary, in combination with local or district public trusts, and aided by a Government guarantee; and that the Executive Council be requested to take all reasonable measures to secure early legislation upon the subject." A resolution in favour of a Royal Commission to consider whether our present system is the best possible system of conduct- ing our railway, and whether it might not ultimately be for the advantage of trade and the benefit of the State that they should become national property was defeated by a large majority. Alderman Bennett gave a very , comprehensive resunui of the business done at the meet- ing of the Association which however contained matters oittle general interest. On the motion of Mr. KnioooN, seconded by Mr. JOHN FAIRCLOUGH, the secretary was cordially con- gratulated on his recent appointment as chairman of the Education Committee, a similar compliment being paid to the deputy-chairman (Alderman Pierpoint), who has for many years been prominently associated with the Chamber.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

The usual meeting was held on Sunday last when there was a fair attendance. Mr. R. Mills presided and spoke of the great pleasure the meetings gave him, and wished more men of the town would come and share in the services. Mr. Walter Hibbert, of Manchester, was the speaker and spoke upon "Thirsting for God." As the body had its thirst, so the soul thirsted for God. Let us try to remove, said he, all hindrances to the satisfy- ing of the soul's great thirst. Let us cultivate a thirst for everything noble, lovable and generous. Mr. Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ. Miss Sumner saw, sweetly "Rest" and "There is a green hill." Mr. C. Cotton also took part in the meeting. The Secretary welcomed five new members total now, 438 for the first month.—The Sick Club members met on Monday night last for their annual meeting, and after meeting all liabilities, agreed to divide ss. to each member who had not received during the year sick-pay for more than three weeks.—The Savings Club has already about 130 s. deposited from between 70 and 80 depositors belonging to the P.S.A. brethren.—Wycliffe P.SA. is in a healthy condition.

CORONER'S APPEAL TO M.P.'s.—ACT WANTED TO SAVE 800 CHIDREN'S LIVES A YEAR.

"I am almost tired of talking about these burning fatalities, but I shall keep at it until something is done to prevent them," said Coroner Brighouse, at St. Helens on Tuesday. It was the old, story of an unprotected fire, even the fender having been placed on one side to keep it clean. Fireguards were cheap enough for poor people in these days, the Coroner went on. Over 800 children lost their lives every year simply through Iparents neglecting to take the ordinary precautions from fire. If it was a horse' a pig, or a dog, all sorts of precautions were adopted, but children were allowed to wander about houses with unguarded fires. Sooner or later there must be legislation. "If members of Parliament would for one afternoon devote their time to the passing of a new Act, making it an offence to leave children in a room where there is an unguarded fire, and, at the same time, make it an offence for a woman to go to bed drunk and take her infant with her, much good would be done and many valuable lives saved.

Jack Porteous, the manager of the South Kensington branch of Hetherington's Bank. was a tall, bearded, broad-shouldered young fellow of thirty-one, who had risen to his present position by sheer hard work and an extraordinary capacity for management and. organisation. His face was deeply lined for so young a man, and those who said that he was scarcely old enough to be in his present position were ignorant of the fact that for the last eight years he had compressed work of two years into every twelve months. The face was a handsome one, but it was aot a pleasant face to look upon, even when it had brightened somewhat after the long draught of whisky-and-soda. A close ob- server would have said it was the face of a man who had sold all happiness for the sake of success. CHAPTER L THE TEMPTRESS

MRS. Gs LA MOTHR sat alonc in her pretty drawing-room in Kensington. A single standard lamp with a fluffy pink shade threw a rose-coloured light on her face. A book lay open on her knees, but it was evident that her thoughts were far away from its oontents. She was twining , her hands to- gether nervously, and the jewelled, rings flashed GA her thin white fingers. Every row and then he looked at the little ormolu clock on the mantelpiece. , Marie de la, Mothe was a beautiful woman; there were those who thought her the most beautiful woman. in the whole wide world. She was not yet thirty, and the contour of her head and features had been pronounced by the most eminent sculptor of the day to he perfect. On this particular evening they seemed almost too perfect too " icily regular, splendidly null." The face was motionless, save for the large restics3 eves, as still as though it had been cut out Of a' block of marble. She was wont to move through the world with that .cold, white face. Yet more than one man could tell to his cost how it had softened, and how the tender glow of love had crept into the flash- ing eyes; aye, and could tell, too, how the features had hardened again e he had pleaded in vain and poured out wild words from a broken heart.

And Marie de la Mothe's vocation in life was to play with hearts, and break her toys when they ceased to amuse ',ler or when they ceased to be profitable. She had been a widow for five years. Some said that Mr. de la Mothe was still alive; others took him on trust ; a few were rather doubtful whether he had ever existed at all. But all agreed on one point that his widow cherished no particular, affection for his memory. She new the world's opinion of her, and smiled on it contemptuously. In one sense of the word Mrs. de la Mothe was a "good woman." Mrs. Grundy, in whose eyes there is only one kind of -virtue, could have inspected her book of life and found therein no blot of any serious con- sequence. But judged by a higher and less narrow standard of morality this beautiful woman was bad to the very core. She had never erred through love, yet many had erred through love ,of her. She moved on, calm and scatheless, through a storm of passions and left behind her a broad track of misery and despair, and even death.

There was no sound In the room but the soft spluttering of the fire in the grate and the ticking of the clock on the mantelpiece. The door opened, and a neat maidservant brought•in tea. Mrs. de la Mothe rose, and went over to the window. The rain pattered against the glass. It was a miserable Novem- ber afternoon. A dull yellow mist shrouded the square on the other side of the road. A line of iron railings and a few gaunt, leafless trees loomed out in the light of the street- lamps. The pavement glistened, and every- thing seemed to drip as though it had been baeed in tears. She turned away from the melancholy spectacle, and regarded the tea-table with satisfaction. It was li.ze looking from a vision of death to all that made life worth living. " Draw the blinds and curtains, Lucy," she said, quietly, crossing over to the fire- side, and settling herself in a low chair. The maid shut out the last dim light of the miserable autumn day, and, moving to the door, stood there as though expecting some further orders.

"Oh, yes," Mrs. de la Mothe said, mechanic- ally. "Tell Mrs. Mixon that tea is ready." In scarcely more than a minute Mrs. Mixon appeared. She was a lady of about fifty, and rustled like a withered leaf. In appear- ance she was tall, angular, and formidable, the personification of respectability, a chaperone par excellence, paid in proportion to her severity. Yet in reality she was a creature as mild as milk, a household drudge, whose work was less noble than that of the sculler Y-maid ; for she had to pretend to be that which she could never be—the stern guardian of Mrs. de la Mothe. She sat down by the tea table and dis- coursed with feeble persistence. Her em- ployer did not trouble to answer her with more than an interrogative " Yes" or "No." and whenever the conversation grew too brisk she offered her a piece of cake or bread- and-butter. In all cases this proved an effective remedy. Mrs. Mixon ate heartily, and, Of course, she could not speak when her mouth was full. .

r Then he opened the door and passed out Into the darkness. Mrs • de la. Mothe watched his tall figure disappear through the, curtain of mist and rain. Then she returned to the drawing. room, and there in the firelight she planned out a fairy edifice of future happiness, where all the world would be glad with love. The sordid past had faded from her mind, and, looking forward, she only saw the 'glorious sunrise of a new life in a new land. _ Little did she dream what fate had in store for both of them. She did not remem• ner that the wages of sin is death.

CHAPTER H. CHE HAND OF TtlE DbAD.

WHEN 'Jack Porteous had gone a dozen yards he looked back through the fog and saw a dark figure silhouetted against a patch of lights. Then he heard the hall-door dose. And, turning up the collar of his coat, he trudged homewards along the wet pave- ment.

Every now and then two bright yellow eyes came out of the mist and glared at him, and a husky voice hailed him from the dark- ness. But he turned a deaf ear to the per- suasions of the cabmen, and continued his course on foot. • He needed physical exer- cise. His brain was .a whirlpool of tumultu- ous thoughts, and he did not wish to be alone with them in the quiet of, a hansom. He liked to feel the rain against his face and to ,grope his way through the cold fog unaided. The very physical discomfort was a tonic to his overwrought nerve.. But nothing could subdue the fire that ' was burning in his brain. The present was a confusion of wild joy and vain regret. '•The' future was, uncertain—now bright with hope, now dark with fear. The past alone was definite—a clearly-cut record of dis- honour and infidelity. He saw it all before him. The sordid I dulness of his home life. The daily quarrels with his wife. • The first meeting with Mrs. 1. de la Motile. The fascination of the woman. The desire to save her from being turned oat penniless into the streets. The first theft. The continual cry for money en some pre- text or- other. The other thefts, made easy . by the responsibility of his position. The gradual passing of friendship into love. The cEmax—reached on that very night. How clearly he saw it all ! How plain was the disgraceof it all ; and yet—he felt that he had received more than he had given. At last he reached hi.s own home. The very exterior; solid, dingy, and respectable, was repulsive to him. In his present mood it seemed to him that he was like a man who, stumbling through the darkness and seeking for light, has suddenly run crash into a blank wall. He paused at the foot of the steps leading up to the Pillared portico, and stood irresolute. A light shone in a bedroom window on, the second floor. The rest of the house was in darkness. He remembered, with an unreasonable feeling of irritation, that his wife was very economical with the gas. The house seemed even more cheerless than the wet fog outside. The very street lamp seemed a gay illumination compared to the thin yellow glow from the bedroom window. Even the hall gas had not been lit. The kitchen was in darkness. The cat, mewing pitifully in the area below, seemed to repre- sent .the entire life of the household.

He looked once more at the severe frontage of the house. There was the sound of a heavy step in the distance. PrAbably it was a policeman ; no one else would move so leisurely on such a miserable night. He did not wish to be seen. Hitherto, the police- man had been a friend watching over his interests as a householder. But to-night Porteous looked at him in a new light. The natural enemy of a thief. The symbol of the laws of society which he had outraged. _ .

_ . He turned abruptly on his heel, and walked down the street. Then he hailed .a passing hansom and drove to his club. There he had a hot bath, and dressed leisurely fox dinner. His present life necessitated his keeping a suit of dress-clothes away from home. When he came down to the smoking. room he ordered a sherry-and-bitters, and lit a cigarette. He nodded to a few acquaint• ances. and sat . down. The flames roared cheerily, and be lay back in the deep. mirocco- covered chair with a sigh' of relief. The outlook seemed more rosy in the warmth and light. For the last few years this club had represented his home life. Here he had sought rest from the nagging tongue al his wife. Here he had been able to think out his ambitious plans for the future. He smiled grimly as he thought of the splendid castle be had built. It had proved but a house of cards, and a woman's hnger had sent it toppling to the ground. He enjoyed his dinner thoroughly that night. The club was famous for its cooking, and its wines were superb. Afterwards a friend came over to his table, and the two men chatted over their coffee and cigars. The friend suggested a music hall and the bank manager fell in readily with the idea/ It was his last evening as Jack Porteous. On the morrow he intended to have his beard and moustache shaved off. From that hour he would be Mr. Hubert Baring. He had decided to take a room at the Cecil and have everything sent there. A letter to the bank that night would explain his absence. He would enclose the keys of the safe. His assistant would see that everything went smoothly. No Suspicion would arise in the minds of any of his subordinates. He was known to be an upright man, and one with whom it was advisable to keep on good terms. The whole of the next. day would be spent in completing his purchases and making arrange• ments for his voyage to South America. He had resolved to cash L. 1,000 of the notes openlyat the Bank of England, and get rid of as many of the others as he could at the' various shops. The whole scheme was simple and apparently . safe. It would be hard to trace the man who cashed the notes a week after ,he bad sailed. Yes, to-morrow he uould be Hubert Baring. It would be the first day of his new life, . But to-eight he was Jack Porteous and he made up his mind to enjoy himself. .

The two men decided to go to the Empire. and they passed a very pleasant evening in the lounge. Afterwards they drove back to the club and stayed there for over an hour. Then Jack Porteous took a cab hoine. The rain had stopped, but the fog had grown more dense, and the drive was long and wearisome. At last the cabman found the house. Porteous paid . him hanelsomely, and mounfed the steps with unsteady feet. He had had a considerable amount to drink that evening. The house was in absolute darkness. The Fog was so dense' that he could only just see the nearest street lamp.. It was no more than a dim yellow circle of light. He heard the muffled beat of the horse's hoofs as his cab drove slowly, away. He leaned against the pillar, and listened till the sound died away in the distance. Then all was silence.

For quite five minutes he did not move. A sudden and unecnquerable fear had seized him. He ,felt that he dare not enter the house. In his oWn drunken imagination the dark walls seemed like a tomb. His soul cried out for light. The fog was like a funeral pall. He turned and fixed his eyes on the one yellow glimmer below. It suggested a distant light- house—a hope and guide for the storm-tossed mariner. He cowered against the pillar, and ciutched the stone with one hand.

Then suddenly from the distance came the sounds of a piano playing "Home. Sweet Home" with infinite variations. The music oroke the spell that was upon him. He drew isimself upright, and listened, Then be lasighed bitterly and looked at the wall ot stone that towered above him, Home, sweet hornet _

THE EXAMINER:

APRIL Bth. 1905.

BRIGHTON.

The Brightou result

does not diminish

the credit of the Government, for the simple reason that the Government has no credit left to be diminished. But it does emphasise, in the most striking way, the nation's resentment. There has never been any such position in our politics before. We have no Government, in the sense of having the authority of the nation for its existence. We have merely a group of irresponsible persons, clinging desperately to places and salaries after it has been made clear beyond dispute that no shred of moral authority remains for their con- tinued quartering of themselves upon a country that is in revolt against them. Every day that they remain in office is a gross abuse of the whole spirit of the Constitution. Not one of their partisans has even the air of denying that the opinion of the country is clear and emphatic. Their continuance comes perilously lacer to public dishonesty. They can only hold on by ignoring the House of Commons as well as the ccuntry. They scuttle from the discussion of the questions in which the nation is most deeply interested ; and, apparently under the impression that they are doing a rather smart thing, only increase the contempt with which reasonable men regard their policy, and emphasise the fact of their abdiction from every- thing except salaries. The Premier's organised boycott of Parliament, and his systematic &grads, time of the representative assembly that stands for the liberty and self-government of the country, has lost for his party one of the supposed safest seats in England. Mr. LODER was notoriously made a Minister less on his own merits—which like those of many members of the Government,are quite unknown either to Parliament or to the constituencies—than on the assumption that he had a safe seat where the risk of a bye-election ' could be faced with some measure of assuranceo The election was rushed with the intention of

if...,,;.:!',.:V5.',.......,,..'A,`,77 ,_,,.,,....,.. , .. - .....,_

giving the Liberals no chance of preparation for it. And it is from this constituency, deliberately selected by tho Premier himself as the one place where he dare face the electors, that his new Minister is rejected by a large:majority.There is one final gratification for Liberals in this result. Mr. BALF3IIR has been clinging to office in the hope that something might turn up to modify public opinion. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S avowed anticipation is that the Liberal party may be returned at the next election with a weak majority. The longer the Government outrages public opinion ,by staying on in defiance of the country's clearly expressed wish, the less likelihood is there of Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN'S anticipation being realised. Instead of improving, the position of the Government worsen, at each time of asking; and every week of delays will, we believe, add to the strength of the Liberal majority when the reckoning comes. There are some things which English public opinion—which with all its faults is a good sporting public opinion, with an instinctive feeling for playing the game—resents far more than it resents a mere difference of opinion. And the revolt against the Government is due in a large measure to the fact that Mr. RALFOUR'S methods have roused this deeper quality of resentment. It is possible to disagree with a GoVernment and still to respect it. That happily has been the general possibility in English politics. What the present Government has done is to sacrifice the respect of the country as well as its agreement in opinion.

Owing to the appeal case in the House of Lords between the Corporation and traders of Warring. ton and the Ship Canal Company the Council meeting has been postponed until next Tuesday. The hearing of the appeal necessitated the presence in London of the Mayor and Town Clerk, aqd, moreover, the estimates of expenditure to be snb• mitted to the Council may be affected. An extra thousand pounds for law expenses was included in the estimates prepared a week ago, and if the Ship :Dana' case can be settled in the meantime possibly a smaller sum will falfil all conceivable requirements.

An Aldermanic vacan q has, of course, been caused by the death of the late Alderman Sutton, and it will be the Council's first duty on Tuesday to elect a successor. The "favourite " for the post is Councillor David Tinnion, who has sat con, tinuausly since 1895, and by seniority and the virtue of services rendered is entitled to promotion. If the seniority rule is adhered to in future elec- tions it will go far towards restoring the balance of political power among the exalted ranks of the Aldermen. At present, Alderman Pierpoint is the only Liberal Alderman, but the seniority rule will confer the next five vacancies on members of the same political per suasion unless members vote in violation of the written agreement. The fact that the Liberals stand to gain so much throuai seniority is a pure accident and in course of time matters would probably work round the other way again, but for the time beiiig it will be a pleasant change to see the Aldermanic honours more equitably shared.

Our remarks last week in reference to the sub- committee's report on the vacant Deputy Town Clerkship have aroused considerable discussion in both professional and Commercial circles. We have every reason to believe that the consensus of opinion in the town is against the sub-committee's proposals. It is generally admitted that, though the Council could probably obtain a qualified solicitor who would undertake the onerous duties specified for £175 a year, they are not likely to get a suitable man for the post. Tnere are probably young solicitors who would be glad of the situation for a time, but it is not to the town's interest that the market should be cheapened in this way. The Deputy Town Clerk will be a responsible official, and we hope the Council will offer snob induce- ments as will secure the services of a man possess- ing not merely the bare legal qualifications but also those of character and attainment.

The opening of the new Free Church Hall at Winwick is the gratifying consummation of an effort which from the very outset !deserved well. Nonconformists in the ancient village will no longer be in the unsatisfactory position of having no place of worship of their own, and it is indica- tive of the spirit of unity which is now happily characterising Free Church work that the members of practically all local Nonconformist denomina- tions have joined in a scheme to meet the common need. The hall promises before long to become the centre of "religious activity, the Free Church Council having arranged for services each Sunday evening during the pre- sent month. Toe impressive discourse by Pro. fessor J. Hope Moulton was in every respect worthy of a memorable occasion, and the day's proceedings certainly inspired confident hopes that the venture will prove of the highest value and profit to the neighbourhood.

The Blackpool Corporation and Magistrates have combined to bring into force a necessary and commendable by-law, which prohibits children under twelve years of age from attending the theatres and variety halls in the town unless accompanied by parent or guardian. A similar enactment would be of considerable service in Warrington, in fact its general adoption would tend to the improvement both of the morale and physique of the rising genera- tion. Youngsters every where seem to go :to the theatre almost as soon as they can toddle, and without either let or hindrance. The theatre is not at all a fit and proper place for, the de- velopment of the juvenile mind, and its in- fluence must be specially harmful if it is, as may be reasonably supposed, responsible for the young patrons keeping late hours. There is already a fairly large number of bye- laws governing child-life in our large towns, but we know of none more sensible than the one which Blackpool has just introduced. When boys and girls have left school and become wage-earners— then, and not before, is the time to indulge in theatre-going if inclination pulls that way.

Should persons receiving Poor Law relief he disfranchised ? So complex did the question appear to the members of the Warrington Board of Guardians that they hesitated to express them- selves in favour of Mr. Charles Schawnn's Bill to remove the present disqualification, and a definite decision on Mrs. Wilkinson's motion was deferred. It is to the credit of most of the speakers that they approached the subject in a very reasonable spirit. Thero is no questioning the injustice of the system whereby the man who through stress of circum- stances has to avail himself fo temporary out-relief, is deprived of his vote for at least t valve months. The law certainly needs amending to meet such cases, but the difficulty which appeared to present itself to the Board was as to where the line must be drawn. To our mind the course is quite clear. If out-relief is wisely administered, the deserving poor will be the recipients and surely such persons are entitled to exercise the franchise. There was an impression abroad that permanent inmates of the House would be affectA but, according to Mrs. Wilkinsons' motion, the tenor of the Bill will be " that no parson shall be deprived of any right to any franchise by reason only that he or any mem- ber of his . family has received out-relief" The argument that me matter is solely one for politicians can nardly be taken seriously, for Guar- 1 dians of the Poor, with their special knowledge, should feel as deeply concerned about it as any 1 section of the community.

-‘ The worst street in Warrington." By common consent the Warrington magistrates on Monday awarded this unenviable distinction to Dial-steeet. It must be admitted, however, that one or two streets in other parts of the town are rather close competitors for this very doubtful honour,
