NOTES AND NOTIONS.

We are told, often and often enough,for fear we for- get that there are four parties in the House of Commons —the Labour Party, the Irish Party, and two others. * * * _

"When did Villiers Brighton?" inquired a corres- pondent of us yesterday. We suppose the answer is: "When be heard that Loder had been Hove."— "Daily News." * * *

Mr. Chamberlain is not coming to Warrington, yet. May we ask the good friends who promised to pay for the meeting to hand over the money they wanted to squander to the Mayor, so that the unemployed may have something to eat? * * *

" Mr. Balfour is tho hardest-worked man in the House. He has a larger and more accurate concep- tion of the duties of a Prime Minister than has beerr at all common in this country, and these duties absorb both time and energy."—" Times."—ls this "kinder sarcastic ?" * *

1, A worthy Wesleyan was fittingly honoured on , Thursday night when the Rev. John Gunnell was made ' the recipient of a hundred guinea testimonial from the Warrington circuit. Mr. Gunnell has for a long time been identified with Penketh, but the warm esteem in which he is held permeates the whole district. We wish him, and Mrs. Gunnell, health and happiness in a well-earned retirement. *s* Local trade unionists will regret to hear that Mr. • Sam Woods who since September, 1894, has been secretary of the Parliamentary Committee of the , Trade Union Congress, intends to retire, and will not offer himself for re-election. Mr. Woods has been itt weak health for some time and has been able to- attend very few meetings of the Parliamentary Com- mittee during the past twelve months. * * * The week after next, Mr. Balfour means to intro- duce the Aliens' Bill in the House of Commons. It is hoped by then that he will be able to find time to be present. Recently he has ignored the debates. They were too irrelevant. Protection is not for discussion in the House of Commons. Two thousand pound buildings in the provinces are deemed more suitable, especially if the audience is carefully selected for the occasion.

** * 6

Two cows which were being driven along Bridge- street on Monday caused no little amusement. When a little way from Market Gate one of the animals came to a standstill and while the man in charge was inducing it to continue its journey the other cow quietly walked into Mr. Atkinson's shop. When part way through the doorway it was met by a coupla of shop-walkers, but after an interval of a few minutes the cow left without buying anything. 107 IM 4 *

Warrington ironworkers are in good spirits this week as the 21 per cent, advance of wages decided upon by the Midland Wages Board has taken effect. The prospects for the ensuing six months, it is stated, are regarded as being most encouraging, as, owing to the superior quality of iron and steel being manufac- tured, there is now a very much increased demand ; some manufacturers having received large foreign orders.

* * * Avenues must henceforth be noted amongst War- rington's attractions. True they ;are not of the leafy order nor are they, as is the case with transatlantic cities, lined with sky-scrapers. The fact is that the market authorities have hit upon the idea of dignifying the walks between the rows of stalls with the applla- tion "Avenue No.—" The arrangement will be con- venient to the officials and 6 tallholders in accurately locating the positions of the stalls. * * *

Still another improvement ! The Fire Station has been equipped with glass-panelled doors and the public now enjoy the advantage of being able to view the engines and apparatus at leisure. The chief pur- pose of the innovation, however, is to facilitate the turn-out of the brigade. The old-fashioned doors were apt to stick awkwardly at times, but in future any risk of that kind will be avoided. The doors are easily released by means of an inside bar and swing outwards in less time than it takes to say so. * * *

Stockton Heath Wesloyans are working hard for the new chapel scheme and it is intended to hold a bazaar in September. Says a writer in the Circuit "Record": "Some of us are sanguine enough to think we can open the New Chapel free of debt, and all would be delighted to secure such an event; it would, indeed, be a red-letter day in our Circuit History. All friends will doubtless help us to attain.. this." We hope the Stockton Heath congregation will have a speedy realization of their ambition! * *

A certain young man with aspirations in many directions has suddenly and mysteriously broken out into song,. His effusion appeared in our Conserva- tive contemporary, and we fain would join in singing it, but somehow the metre is misleading. A friend has sent us a reply which, too, is a trifle awry, but we print it so that he may see for himself how it looks in. cold typo. To W.L.W.xxx,,,s.

Oh! cease thy flutterinp, calm thy troubled breast, No "twaddle lUce tinao own appears- Pity shall soon restore thee to the rest, So dry up, please, dry up thy tears. * *

As an asset of the progressive forces in Warring - ton the Progressive Labour League is already assert- ing its value. The weekly debates are entered into with spirit and afford evidence of the desire of the workingmeh to be well-informed on the great questions of the day. In the arrangement of the Liberal public meetings to be held in various parts of the town neat week the league has co-operated very acceptably and successful gatherings are confidently anticipated. A joint debate with the Social Service League on the unemployed question will be held about a fortnight hence. •* * *

In a f6w days all will be activity in the local Volunteer Corps. For some time several sections have been hard at work practising and nest week the company drills will begin in earnest. Two lectures on" outpost duty" have been delivered during the present week and the ambulance corps, under the direction of Surgeon Major Adams, has commenced work. The members of the signalling section, of which Captain Ilasla.m has charge, are devoting them- selves to duty with special energy as they are ex- pecting to undergo examination this year. The appeal for more recruits for the battalion appears to have called forth a gratifying response. Sergeant Major Rawlinson' who rendered such valuable service to the battalion, has been succeeeed by Sergeant lajor Ingram. * * There was quite an impr3ssive display of good fellowship and mutual congratulation at the meeting of the Board of Guardians on Wednesday when the members joined in expressing their cordial thanks to the chairman (Hr. A. Brooks) for his services during the past year. One was almost irresistably reminded of a certain humorous trio commencing We never have stormy meetings or call each other names.'' Joking apart, it is a gratifying feature of public life that despite pronounced differences of opinion oil vital questions, the members of local authorities are on such occasions broad-minded enough to unite in recognizing the merits of the colleague who has devoted his abilities to guiding the year's delibera- tions. Mr. Brooks is certainly deserving of the unstinted compliments bestowed upon him. *

"There is no doubt that a large wave of psychisin is just now passing over the world," so writes Mrs. Campbell Praed " The Occult Review." It is quite evident that she, too, has heard of the young man from Wales named Evan Roberts. What a wonderful study he is to be sure ! On Tuesday evening last be went to Liscard to talk to the people over there and he said some funny things. One remark particularly struck us. It hurt us to the quick. " Oh! that God would send down Ms angel from heaven to pick out all the hypocrites here !" We began to wonder how many would be left of us. II" God would send down his angel" to remove all those who I maks hypocrites of his people there would then be. a. thanes for the Social Reformer to make more rapid progress. We do not doubt but that Mr. Roberts doing good in certain quarters but we should be sorry to see "emotionalism "mistaken for Christianity.

CYCLES at 12 guineas with change speed gem &c., unequalled.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge St., Warrington.—Advt. ISt. John's Church. The Literary and Social Society in connection with St. John's concluded the 1904-5 session by the hold- ing of a social gathering in the schoolroom on Tues- day evening last. The hon. president, the Rev, J. L. Milne, 8.D., occupied the chair, and there was a fair muster of members and their fi lends. The evening's programme opened with a piano solo by Miss Wells. Songs were given by Miss Woodland and Mr. Ditch- field, a violincello solo by Mr. Jackson, and humorous Scottish readings by Miss Dewar. The organist, Mr. J. W. Jowett, acted as accompanist. Tea was provided and handed round by the lady members during the evening. UNDER THE CLOCK for Good Boors.—Advt. Mr. R. A. Naylor's Art Treasures. Among the art treasures possessed by Mr. R. A. Naylor, is the little known portrait of William Clarkson Stanfield, R.A., by Sir Thomas Lawrence, P.R.A. According to Ruskin, Stanfield occupied the premier position as a cloud painter, for he describes him as "The leader of English realists, and incompar- ably the noblest master of cloud form of all artists." His position as a scenic artist was possibly unrivalled and the drop scene for "Frozen deep," painted for Charles Dickens's private theatricals, and which was painted by the artist in two days, was sold at the Gads Hill hale for £l,OOO. The portrait in question represents Stanfield when about 30 years of age. It is three-quarter length and shows the artist holding a book in his hand. on which are inscribed the words ; "Sketches by Stanfield." Anyone seeing the hand- some face,' without being a physiognomist, can detect him. Truly Charles Dickens described him in his charming memorial notice in "All the Year Round," June, 1869 :—" The soul of frankness, generosity, and simplicity, the most loving and lovable of men.'" "PREMIER" CYCLES at 9 guineas for Best Grade, unsurpassed.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. Warrington Choir's Success at Leigh. The seventh annual eistedfood at Leigh on Saturday lasted nearly eight hours, and it was well on for eleven o'clock before the result of the principal com- petition of the day, a contest for male voice choirs at 30 to 45 voices was declared. Then, amidst great enthusiasm; it was announced that the Wigan Har- monic Chorus had carried off the first price £lO and a silver cup) with 100 marks out of a possible 105. Warringion were second, with 98 marks, and the Anion Prize Glee Union (Nelson) third, with 97. The test piece was De Rifle's " Martyrs of the Arena." The Leigh Wesleyan Choir won the competi- tion for mixed voice choirs the prize being five guineas,and a gold medal to the conductor. The test piece was Stainer's anthem " What are these ? " The Appollo Party (Wigan) won the quartette competition, and Mr. J. Jackson, Ashton- under-Lyne, was successful in the gentleman's challenge solo for which there were 31 entries. Miss G. Blair, of Glazebrook, was the prize-winner in the pianoforte solo competition. Miss L. Fairhurst, War- rington, carried off the first prize in,the contralto solo competition. ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASs NtINERAL WATERS.—Advt. Accidents at the Football Match.

Saturday's match between Warrington and Wigan was happily unattended by any accidents of a serious character though several spectators had narrow escapes. With the object of obtaining a better view of the game a number of enthusiasts climbed on to the roof of the stand of the popular side. P.C. Hewitt followed and in their haste to escape the officer two slipped and fell from the roof to the ground. One alighted on his feet and after satisfying himself that no bones:were broken he walked aivay.The other individual was hardly so fortunate though he has reason to con- gratulate himself on a remarkable escape from injury. , From a height offifteen feet he fell on his head. For a few minutes he lay motionless when to the surprise of two young men who picked him up ho recovered and walked away to view the game from a less danger- ous position. One man, John George Buckley, Winwick-road, was with others seated on the top of a fence when it collapsed. Buckley had a very nasty fall which resulted in his leg being broken. He was conveyed to the Infirmary where his injuries were dressed.

H. M. ASTITON'SI LIFE TONIC is the Krsa, or Toares.-63. 4 v-s MEE r, A arrington.—Advt. Warrington Horse Parade. The report of the Warrington Horse parade Com mittee upon the fourth annual parade, is in every way satisfactory. They are pleased to record an increase in the number of entries, and a continued improves ment in the general appearance of the turnouts. Thi has been perceptably maintained throughout the year, a fact that is particularly pleasing and brings prominently before the public the objects the Committee aim at attaining, viz., to improve the general condition and treatment of horses, and to encourage the drivers to take an interest in the animals under their care. The com- mittee continuel "We are pleased to announce that the President (Sir Gilbert Greenall), has promised to give two silver cups, one for the best light lurry or van horse, and one for the best light horse, and Mr. Peter Davies has kindly promised a Silver Cup to be presented to the owner of the best heavy horse in the procession. The Shire Horse Society have also promised two Silver Medals and a premium of .1:1 for the best pair of horses in double harness and the best horse in single harness. The R.S.P.C.A. Society has again forwarded the diplomas, one for each Class, on the same lines as before. New features this year, are the prizes offered by Lady 'Greenall for cabs and hansoms. which the Committee hope will be keenly contested for:" PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. The Examinet, Saturday, April 8, 1905.

Personal. Mr. T. Birchall, J.P., is still confined to his house with a severe attack of bronchitis. He has been ailing for a long time and his condition is causing his friends considerable anxiety. On Saturday, at Berne, Switzerland, a daughter was born to Lord and Lady Acton. Mother and child are reported to be doing well. Lady Acton will be remembered locally as the daughter of Mr. T. H. Lyon, of Appleton Hall. Major C. J. Holmes, Royal Army Medical Corps (R.P.), has arrived at Warrington, and assumed medical charge of the station hospital and troops at that station, in relief of Lieutenant Colonel C. W. A Tell-Tale Finger Print. About 5.30 on the morning of March !•23rd, a young man gave himself up to the Warrington police with a ' stolen bicycle in his possession. This, it seemed, he had taken from outside the Ship Inn, Rainhill, on the previJus evening, while its owner was making a call within.— Sergeant Baxendell, at Prescott Petty Sessions on Tuesday, said that the, young fellow declined to give any information about himself, and so an impres- sion was taken of one of his fingers, with the result that they had received prisoner's photograph and notice from London that he was Robert Arthur Ellison, a suspicious • character.—Prisoner Was remanded to the Quarter Sessions at Liverpool, to be held on the 18th inst. ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS MLNERAL I ATERS.—Advt. Buckley-street Band of Hope. In connection with Buckley-street Sunday School Band of Hope a grand concert was given on Monday evening by the pupils of Miss L. Williams' School and the Band of Hope Juvenile Choir. Miss Williams' Orchestra rendered two items in a very pleasing manner. The pupils were responsible for the follow- ing, action songs and 'songs and choruses, "The Flowery Garland," "Go Bye-bye," "Tardy Scholar" and " Pit pat." Each item was exceedingly nretty and encores were demanded for all. The duet "Gipsy Countess" snug by Miss Dunn and Master T. Tanner proved to be the most popular item of the evening. Tl.e juvenile dhoir were very successful in the cantata "The Lost Gipsy." The principal parts were well sustained by Miss C: Hewitt as Gipsy, Misses A. Whalley and I. Harrison, Schoolgirls ; Mister A. Fairhurst, Gipsy; Masters P. Kean and A. Palmer, Schoolboys. Mr. A. E. Trow who had trained the choir, accompanied. 'On Tuesday evening the pro- gramme was repeated with equal rsuccess. In addition Mr. Whitfield gave two humorous recitations Miss Williams' pupils rendered "Five O'clock Tea." The part of gipsy in the cantata was ably taken by Miss Ada Fairhurst. Mr. A. Jennings occupied the chair both evenings. Debate on Disestablishment. At the weekly meeting of the Social Service League held in Golborne-street school on Thursday evening under the presidency of the Rev. H. Dannico, a debate on "Should the Protestant State Church be disestab- lished and disendowed ?" was opened by Mr. Joseph Howes. He defined the meaning of the word " Protestant" and pointed out that the age of the Established Church depended upon that definition, because if it were a Protestant Church—as he con- tended, according to Acts of Parliament, it was—it could not have existed before the protest was made. Mr. Howes alluded to the Acts of Par- liament passed from the end of the reign. of Henry VIII. down to the Act of Uniformity showing, as he submitted, how the Established Church came into existence. Dealing Nvith disendowment he referred to the origin and history of some of the State Church revenues, pointing out how a vast amount of property now under the control of the Eciclesiasticab Commissioners had been obtained on the short lease ' system and that as landlords the Commissioners had acquired a great deal of wealth created by Noncon- formists as well as Churchmen, but which was now used exclusively for the benefit of one religion. He argued ag”inst State interference in religious matters suggesting that true religion was too sacred for Parliament to touch. Disestablishment, he further cmtended, was impossible without disendowment. The discussion was taken part in by the Chairman, Councillor Magian, Messrs. Hawthorne, Heywood, E. Wynne Jones, Elwell and Dalby, the opiniori being almost unanimous in favour of disestablishment. With regard to endowments, the Rev. H. Dunnico sub- mitted that money left to the church since the Reformation might wisely be left in the bands.of the Anglican Church while that left prior to the Reforma- tion, which now really belonged to the State, could be well applied to educational purposes. Mr. Howes was thanked on the motion of Mr. Morton seconded by Mr. Hitchin.

Another Appointment.

use their powers to the very utmom _.aey might be sure that when they were working with Him they were getting the very utmost possible out of their lives. That principle worked in the very composition of the Word of God. The Bible gave as much trouble to the writers as any secular book could have done. What was called inspiration was sinply that human scribes, prophets, seers and historians, were engaged in writing to the very best of their powers, and God came down upon them and filled them with that which had made the Book not only absolutely human but absolutely divine. That afternoon they were gathered together under circumstances which made them feel that surely the Lord Himself must have built that house,and there- fore these who had toiled for it could not possibly have toiled in vain. They were thinking at the present time as they read even the secular newspapers of the great 'subject of the revival of God's work that was so blessedly visible in many parts of this country as well as in Wales. They were asking what it was that man had to do in preparation for such a revival of God's work. He thought they might go back to their text or to the well-known story of Elijah on Mount Carmel, to see what they had to do. r t was their business to build the altar, to lay the victim in place, to make all the promises, and then to pray, and down from Heaven above would come the sheet of flame with which God answered whenever men had dedicated themselves in earnest prayer to His service, whenever men had resolved that no longer would they be hindrances to God working out His own acts and achieving His own grand purposes in His own way. They prayed that soon the sacred fire might come down upon the house in which they were met that afternoon to worship God. The Divine teacher, concluded the preacher, led them by ways which were sometimes hard to understand, through happiness and sorrow, success and failure, but there was no sorrow, that was to be regarded as ultimate sorrow; there that was no failure that was to be regarded as true failure for him who had dedicated his life to God. A time was coming when the problem would be solved, and in the light of the after knowledge they would know that all the time One who was too wise to err had been leading them perpetually towards the great end He had in view for their lives. The Rev. J. F. Matthews expressed on behalf of the Free Church Council and members of that fine congrega- tion their extreme gratitude to Professor Moulton. In a short address Professor Hope Moulton pointed out that the Free Church Council had not come to the village in order to show hostility to anybody. Even if the relations between the Established Church and the Free Churches in those parts were absolutely ideal, there was abundance of room in any village for the two kinds of services. ousted, and thae in any event the Canal Company had waived their rights to insist on arbitration. The more confirming of anarbitration clause had never been taken by the court to be conclusive as to ousting jurisdiction. The court had always taken all the circumstances into view, and it was a question of construction in each individual case, whether having regard to the whole statute and the circumstances of the case, the particular clause ousted jurisdiction or not. If there was a general rule it would be that an arbitration clause in a public Act of Parliament did oust the juris- diction of the court, but that a mere arbitration clause in a private Act did not oust jurisdiction. There was nothing in the agreement in this case to carry it further than that. It was clearly an agreement made pursuant to the power in the original Act•to modify if they pleased the original statutory agreement. The arbitra- tion clause was clearly part of the agreement, and the parties were entitled to waiver, and it only con- ferred upon either party the right to insist upon a special tribunal. He asked their lordships to hold that the reasoning in the Court of Appeal was not sound and that there was a right to go to the courts to enforce the agreement qua agreement. Mr. Moulton, K.C., on behalf of the Manchester Ship Canal Company, said no doubt a difficulty arose through there being two statutes and two parties—the Corpora- tion and the traders—whose case might be, and he thought was, diffaent to a certain extent. The statutory obligation of dredging the channel of the Mersey at the point in question was by the Act of 1895 placed on the Manchester Ship Canal Company as between the Corporation of Warrington and the com- pany. Had that dredging been done clearly under both ' Acts of 1885 and 1896? That was a question which could only be decided by an arbitrator or engineer, and if that was how the matter had to be decidedas between the Corporation and the company it was surely most improbable that the traders were to be allowed , to go to a diflarent tribunal—namely, the courts of law, I which might have taken an entirely different view from the arbitrator. Mr. Cripps, K.C., followed on the same side. Mr. Neville, K.C., being heard for the appellants in reply, • The Lord Chancellor intimated that their lordships would reserve judgment. The question to be decided now is whether the dredging dispute should have been referred to an arbi- trator in the first instance, instead of being submitted to a court of law.

THE EVENING PROCEEDINGS.

Vigorous Speech by the Rev. J. F. Matthews.

At the evening meeting there was a 'crowded attend- ance the seating capacity of the building being taxed to the utmost. Mr. G. T. Oatley, of Bristol occupied the chair, being supported by the Revs. J. Yonge, J. S. Hughes, J. F. Matthews, H. Wall and A. Harvey, (Widnes), and Messrs. C. E. Parker and Thomas Dale. The Rev. J. F. Matthews said that roughly speaking they had in hand or promised the sum of £335, and they reckoned that the collections and offering that day would amount to £2O. Mr. Ormiston had informed him that he had £lO more so that they had some £lOO Already given or promised towards the cost in round figures of £7OO for the completion of the scheme. He thought that a..very fair start. ((fear, hear.) He was delighted to be able to say that the spirit which had characterised those who had the work in hand had been very generous and happy. As Free Churchmen The thought they were to be warmly congratulated upon having opened such a building in a village in which hitherto Free Church services had never been held. He hoped tho building would be intimately associated with the furtherance of the Eree Church movement in the parish. (Applause.) Mr. Matthews proceeded to refer to a speech made by the Rev. J. Yonge at the foundation stone laying ceremony, in which the Rev. Yonge said the building was not being erected in a spirit of antagonism to any existing church. These words had been uttered, he said, with the usual 'urbanity and courtesy which always characterized Mr. Yonge and could not have been better said by anyone. (Hear, hear.) Yet he had been a little it troubled mime he read those words, not because anyone who beard them uttered might have any misunderstanding, but because they might have been construed into something of the nature of an apology for their entrance into the village as Free Churchmen. Lie did not think they were in any sense meant as an apology. In fact the time for apology for Free Church

CRAWFORD'S GOLDEN BUTTER

BISCUITS.

PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE.

Debate on Education.

An interesting debate on the education question was introduced by Councillor John Maginn at the weekly meeting in connection with the Progressive Labour League held at the Liberal Club on Tuesday evening. Mr. S. Wright presided over a good attendance. Councillor MAGINN contended that working men, no matter to which denomination they belonged, were I bearing an unfair burden of the educational expendi- ture. The Education Act vas carried through the I House of Commons in opposition to the great majority of the people of the country, and be considered that a question of such vital importance should have been submitted to the elector- ate before the Bill was introduced. Education, in his view, should be an imperial and not a local matter as it was in the interests of the whole community that children should be well taught. The taxes for educa- tion should be collected imperially and each individual should bear an equal share. If education charges in the town continued to increase at the present rate it would have the effect of depopulating working centres. The present system of rating was unequally distributed, as the working people in towns had to pay for the education of a larger number of children than the well- to-do classes residing in the country. Workinmen had also to contribute to the cost of secondary education from which most of them obtained no benefit because they could not afford to send their children to the higher institutions. To be of advantage to the working class community, secondary education must take the form of equipping the children to earn their living. Children should be educated to the highest level of ' manufacturing on scientific lines at the expense of the state. A spirited debate was taken part in by the Chairman, the Rev. H. Dunnico, Messrs. G. Walker, E. Pliuston, Hitchin, Cotterell. Dailey and others. Mr. WALKFiIa thought that to make education an imperial charge would mean a great loss of interest in the question on the part of the general mass of the community, as the taxation would be indirect. The ratepayers of Warrington were now paying through the nose for what ought to have been done years ago. Had there been a School Board about ten years ago new schools would have been erected and the result would have been tin educational system worth calling a municipal one. As it was we had twenty or thirty schools, and scarcely one of them was modern. The Education Committee had converted a chaotic system into something like regularity. He had been taken to task by the local teachers for some recent remarks, but he repeated that it was quite possible to fix the educational ideal too high, so that while it berm-: fitted a few it was of practically no use to the great majority. The Rev. H. DuNNrco agreed that education could I never be efficient and adequate until it was national. There ought to be more attention given also to an elementary system of education, for, he contended, that to a very large degree the duty of the State ended when it had given to the child a sound elementary basis of education. It was unfair to tax the poorer classes for secondary education which only in very few instances could they avail themselves of. If they wanted to provide a higher education for the cleverer children of the poorer classes it could be done by means of bursaries and scholarships. He contended that the ratepayers did not get an adequate return for the expenditure on the Secondary School at Warrington as the children educated there were very largely the children of those who were well able to pay. He agreed that the ultimate solution of the education difficulty would be to raise the whole cost by imperial taxation, but the control should remain in the hands of local authorities He defied anyone to put to a better method than the old School Board system. Mr. PLINSTON said they ought to make it possible for every child of ability to attain the topmost ring of the 1 educational ladder, but they must not force education to a degree that was not necessary to enable the work- ing classes to gain a living. A vote of thanks to Councillor Maginn concluded th meeting.

At Sotheby's a first folio Sbakspere was, after close bidding, knocked down to Messrs. Sonoran for £255. The volume was included in the library of the late Mr. Abott, C. 8., of Ayrshire. The preliminary pages had been supplied in facsimile, but otherwise the folio was in a good state.

WARRINGTON'S

NEXT AMATEUR OPERA. "The Mikado."

THE CAST AND THE CHORUS.

Public curiosity has naturally been whetted as to the neat opera which Members of the Warrington Musical Society intend to present to their almost innumerable patrons. We are sure the selection of "'he Mikado " will give general satisfaction, for there is little doubt that if a popular vote were taken this delightful opera would be found to take high rank among the favorite Gi lbert-Sullivau collaborations. En- couraged by the splendid success which has attended the two previous operatic productions, the Committee has decided to afford the public extended opportuni- ties of seeing this yem's performances by presenting "The Mikado " in the Parr Hall for six nights, commencing October 10th. The first rehearsal will be held in Cairo Street Schoolroom on Tuesday, May 2nd, and there is every indication that the pro- ject will be entered into with that whole-heartedness which has been so important a factor in the attain- ment of past successes. The cast, as decided upon at a recent meeting of the Opera Committee, is a powerful one. While most of the principals who have already achieved triumphs in the amateur operatic realm will make welcome re-appearance, the public will be interested in the inclusion for the first time it) leading parts of two well known lady vocalists. CUTTINGS FROM THE MAGAZINES.

THE LION IN GREECE.

Some time ago Professor A. B. Meyer, the Director of the Zoological Museum at Dresden, published an article un the alleged existence of the lion in historical times in Greece. A translation of this article appears in the recently issued Annual Report of the Smithsonian Institution. As regards the mention of that animal in Homer, the author is of opinion that the writer of the Iliad was probably acquainted with the lion, but this does not prove its former existence in Greece. The accounts given by Herodotus and Aristotle merely go to show that about 500 B.C. lions. existed in some part of Eastern Europe. The Greek name for the lion is very ancient and this suggests, although by no means demonstrates, that it refers to an animal indigenous to the countfy.• Although fossil bones of the lion have been recorded,no recent remains of that animal are known from Greece; but this cannot be regarded as a matter of any import- ance in connection with the question. On the whole, although the evidence is not decisive, it seems probable that lions did exist in Greece at the time of Herodotus ; and it is quite possible that the representation of a , lion-chase incised on a Mr:enean dagger may have been ' taken from life. In prehistoric times the lion was spread over the greater part of Europe : and if, as is very probable, the so-called Feria atrox be inseparable, its range also included the greater part of North America.--" Knowledge." CONCERNING "SUCKS."

" This is another thing my aunt doesn't know the meaning of," says "The Schoolboy" in the "April Windsor." "She told me to do something one day, and because she jolly well knew I didn't want to do it, she made up her mind that I wonld'nt. (It was to write a letter to a beastly relation.) And when she came prowl- ing along in an hour or two, and found me readirg `Stalky,' she dropped on me without waiting to ask any questions, and gave me an awful wigging for disobed- ience and want of family feeling, all in a lump." "I listened to everything she had to say, and when she wasn't able to think of anything else in the way of jabber, I just handed her the letter, beautifully written (though I say it myself) and ready to be posted." "1 wasn't vine* to descend to a lot of mean person- , alities, as she had done in her abuse of me; so, when I gave it to her, I merely said, 'Sucks to you ! '—which was brief and to the point." "You never saw any one so badly sold as she was, and still she was too big a silly to know what 'sticks meant, although she had just been given what might be called a practical illustration. She said it sounded dis- gusting, and that I was a nasty, dirty little wretch ; but after having enjoyed the satisfaction of giving her such a gorgeous suck, I didn't care how many names she called me. When one knows one has had the best of it, one can afford to be indifferent to mere exhibi- tions of temper on the part of one's elders."—" April Windsor Magazine." HOW THE SUEZ CANAL WAS MADE.

In digging the Suez Canal, the physical conditions I presented no special difficulty. The material MIS mainly sand, with some rook in thin strata near Chalouf, and the greatest elevation to be cut through did not exceed GO feet, and that only for a short distance. The tides are inconsiderable, necessitating no costly controlling works. labour was abundant, and so cheap that it corn- ! peted successfully with mechanical methods for much of the work. In the early stages of the work hand labour I with wheelbarrows and buckets was largely used, and the men oven scooped the mud with their hands. The whole of the cuts through the high ground were taken out in this crude way, the rate of wages being 15 cents to 20 cents per day, without board. All the dredges were of the endless-chain type, and at first the material was deposited into barges and dumped in the lakes or in the set. Soon great difficulty was lencuuntered in getting rid of the stuff, and dredges with a long shoot or "couloir " were devised. In these dredges the material was raised to a height of 40 to 41 feet and dumped into a hopper, whence the spoil was carried ashore in a couloir some 220 feet long. This couloir consisted of a trough or spout carried by la skeleton frame or cantilever of wrought iron, the base of which rested on a separate float alongside the dredge. The couloir had a slope shore wards of about 1 in 10, and the discharge was assisted by a stream of water. and by a chain with scrapers attached which was- caused to travel down the bottom of the couloir. The greatest cliffloulty was to dirpose of the material on the bank satisfactorily, and it is evident that, what- ever the length of the couloir, the deposit of mud, sand, and water, must sooner or later reach a point at which some of the material will run back again into theortnal.— A. W. Robinson, in "The Engineering Magazine" for April. AN INCIDENT OF PIG-STICKING.

The boar is now beginning to realise that with such relentless and determined foes neither his speed, activity, cunning, nor knowledge of the ground will save his skin. He is blowing, lurching, and going in shifty zig- zags: while the Water is now well in hand, and his rider is only awaiting a favourable moment to call on him for a final rush. The moment appears to have come, the spear is levelled for a purposeful thrust, and the good horse answers to hand and knee; but even as the keen blade pierces and enters three inches into his flank the boar wriggles aside from the stinging steel and postpones the fatal moment. It is not a disabling blow, as Mayne realises only too well ; still,it is enough to swear by, and the sight of blood trickling from the upheaved blade and the barely triumphant hout of " Spear' which reaches them are enough however, to do more than rouse all the fire and fury of the dauntless beast without much weakening his forces ; it evokes, too, fell determination not to be speared again with- out playing a card or counterstoke of his own. At the touch of the spear he has swerved to the right, almost in the path of Turner and Barrow, who press deter- minedly in his tracks, the former leading. For a time he travels in short,jerky zigzags,which foil any attempt to close, while fiery vicious eyes are cast backwards on his pursuers. Then with a sudden angry impulse, he jinks sharp to the left, wheels again, and launches himself on the near flank of Turner's Arab like a flash of grey fury. Before horse or spear can be turned to stein his onslaught he is all among the horse's legs, cutting, gashing, and working his wicked will wholesale with cruel upward-thrusting tushes. The Arab lashes out and plunges, but finally comes down on his knees, and Turner must fain dis- mount, while the boar is away again, with . new pur- pose, to meet in full career Barrow, who is sailing down on him, spear in rest. Neither flinches a hair's-breadth from the course, and the spear pierces the boar's chest with deadly accuracy: but the stout bamboo half shivers to splinters in Barrow's grip, and he himself, from the impetus and shock, totters, overbalances, and falls almost on the top of the pig, who, however, now withdraws a pace or two from the wreckage, shaking his head and wildly gnawing on the broken spear-stem. —From "The Spur and Spear," in the "Cornhill Magazine" for April.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

SROPBREAKING AT WARRINGTON.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday William Campbell was charged with breaking into the lock-up shop of the Liverpool "Courier." Winwick-street, Warrington, with intent to steal. Prisoner was caught in the shop by P.C.' s Whitby, Howard and Newey, shortly after midnight on Saturday. He had forced the back door and was busy ransacking the shop. Under ordinary circumstances some £l.O would have been on the premises but fortunately the manager of the branch had taken the money home with him. Prisone was remanded until Thursday.—On Thursday, Campbell was again brought before the magistrates when the charrre was reduced to one of stealing a pocket book from the lock-up shop of the "Liverpool Courier."—Mr. Steel on behalf of the prisoner made a strong plea for leniency. Campbell was the son of respectable parents and had a wife and two little children.—Superintendent Monaghan said the prisoner had been previously con- victed of shopbreaking. lie was a man who would not work.—The Chief Constable said there had been a num- ber of cases of shopbreaking recently. He desired to press the charge.—Prisoner was sent to goal for three months with hard labour.

APPOINTMENT OF RELIEVING OFFICER.

The Board unanimously decided to appoint Mr. Pearson,Grappenhall to the position of relieving officer for the Frodshara district in succession to Mr. Riley. The Chairman in informing Mr. Pearson of the decision of the Board said they hoped he would be a good servant. They would do their best to make the post as comfortable as possible. Mr. Pearson said he appreciated the honour the Council had done him and could assure them that he would serve the Board to the best of his ability.

THE LATE ALDERMAN SUTTON.

Pulpit References.

Preaching at St. Paul's Church on Sunday morning the Rev. W. Bracecamp made a brief but touching reference to the late Alderman Sutton. Daring the week, he said, they had been overwelmed with grief at the loss they, as a congregation had sustained ; in fact, the town of Warrington was poorer to-day for the loss of Alderman Thomas Sutton. He had been intimately connected with that church 13 years as people's warden and two years as vicar's warden, and, though not in actual office right up to the time of his death, he always took a keen interest in their welfare, and was always ready to help them with his means, and attended the services as often as he could. It was a very long time since there was such a funeral in Warrington as the one on Friday, such a representative one of all sects and parties, which was ample proof,if such were needad.of the life and character of one who was so respected and beloved by all. He would be a man greatly missed. by individuals and by the town generally. If the history of Warrington was ever recorded, Thomas Sutton's name must be added to the list of worthies who had helped to make Warrington what she was to day. He was very popular among his colleagues in the Connoil, and his advice was always sound and practicable. Two of their most honoured men, Sir Gilbert Greenall and Mr. J. Charlton Parr, were pleased to recognize him as their "mach esteemed and 31d and valued friend." Such was the life and char- acter of him whom they laid to rest, and for whose loss they mourned that day. It seemed to him that there was a lesson for them all—to try and copy such an example, so that when they were called away others might speak of them as they could speak of him who was gone. At the conclusion of the service the organist (Mr. Wilkes) played the Dead March in" Preaching at the Part Hall on Sunday evening the Rev. H. Minnie() also referred to the loss which the town has sustained by the death of Alderman Sutton. He said that he could not do other than express his sorrow at the removal of one who had devoted the greater portion of a long life to promoting what he con- sidered to be the best interests of his town. Politic- ally, and, in a sense, religiously, Alderman Sutton and he stood on opposing platforms, but that did not prevent him from paying his tribute of respect to the deceased gentleman's memory. On his own behalf and on their bealf he tendered their sympathy to the bereaved family. REFERENCE AT THE BOARD OF GUARDIANS

MEETING. At the meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday, Mr. G. Thompson briefly alluded to the death of Alderman Sutton who was at one time a mem- ber of the Board. He thought it was fitting that the Board should place on record their esteem of the ser- vices rendered to the Borough and to the county by the late Alderman and forward a vote of condolence with the family in their bereavement. He moved a resolu- tion to that effect. Mr. HicKmA.N seconded and the motion was carried.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

CROSFICLD'S WORKS v. TECHNICAL A..N.D CLERICAL STAFFS. This match took place on Monday on the Warrington Albion ground, Liverpool-road. Dr. Markel kicked off and the "staff" at once pressed, scoring in the first few minutes. The " works" broke away but the final shots were wide. Half-time the "staffs" were leading t)y 1 goal to nothing. In the second half the works equalised and it seemed as if the match was going to be drawn, but 15 minutes off time a change came over the game, and the works scored again. The fast nature of the play, had evidently told upon the "staffs" team, and the staying power olf their opponents being better, they put through 3 more goals finally winning by 5 goals to 1.

The Examiner, Saturday, April 8, 1905.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

ST. MARV'S, LATCHFORD.

Solemn Opening of the Organ.

There was a disappointment in store for the large congregations which attended St. Mary's Churoh, Latchford, on Sunday, on the occasion of the solemn opening. after renovation and re-erection, of the new organ. I The Bishop of Shrewsbury had been announced to preach morning and evening, but owing to ill-health his lordship was reluctantly compelled to cancel the engagement. In a letter to the priest-in- charge (Rev. Father Mc Grath) the Bishop said it was always a pleasure to him to meet the good people of Latchford,and he hoped they would understand that he was severely disappointed in not being able to be with them. He should look forward to a visit to Latchford later in the year to redeem his promise. In the Bishop's absence the preacher for the day was the Rev. Father Jagger, S.J., who has a Maid and convincing style of delivery, and he gave two able sermons. In the morning Solemn High Mass was celebrated and although, owing to the Lenten season, the service was of a more subdued character than is usual on special occasions, the rendering of the music by the choir, under Mr. Peter Bate's able conductorship, was most impressive. Mr. John Eckersley, organist of St. Mary's, Battermarket-street, presided at the organ and demonstrated the good qualities of the instrument to advantage. The Mass music selected was "Gounod's Messe du Sacr4 Cceur," and the members of the choir did fall justice to the work. The most effective portion was, without doubt, the Sanctus, in which the voices blended beautifully. The soloists were the Misses Eckersley and Richardson, and Messrs. Joseph M. and Peter Bate. As an offertory piece " Laudate Dominum" (Berneuioni) was rendered with excellent taste, and Mr. Peter Bate is to be complimented upon a highly credit- able intrepretation of the solo. The celebrant of the Mass was the Rev. Father A. H. D'Arcy, the Rev. Father Radcliffe and the Rev. Father D'Arcy acting as deacon and sub-deacon respectively. The altar was beautifully decorated with the choicest flowers. The Rev. Father Jagear devoted his sermon to ex- pounding the doctrine of sacrifice of the Mass. God, he said, had, out of the love he bore to the children of men, given unto them a sacrifice—the Mass—that was fully and adequately -.tisfying to all the claims of divine justice and therein they had an offering that was worthy o.! God. The Mass was the sacrifice pre-figured in the old law, and that it was a sacrifice was clear from the words of institution used by our Lord when He spoke of the giving of His body and the shedding and out-pouring of His blood for many for the remission of ' sins. The Mass was also a sacrifice commemorative of the sacrifice of Christ on Calvary. It was, in other words, Calvary brought home to them. The offering of the Mass did net imply that the sacrifice of Christ on the Cross was in any sense inzomplete, but Christ had chosen the Mass to be a means whereby the merit and grace stored up on the Cross could be applied to the souls of men. Had Christ not given to His Church that sacrifice the Church would have had no one highest act of worship,no one sacrifice that was absolutely adequate to satisfy all God's claims for worship, praise, and I propitiation, As the planets revolved about the sun and received their light from it, so all the Church's praise and worship was centered round the presence of Christ in the Holy Eucharist and devoted to the ministration of that great sacrifice. In celebrating the dedic...tion of the organ that day thoy had to remember that it would be primarily devoted to enhancing the splendour of the offering of the Holy Eucharist. •At the evening service the music was again an out- standing feature.Thesolos without exception were given I with splendid effect Mr. J. Babbitt being heard to especial advantage in "0 Salutaris" No. 24 (Moorat). Other items were " Salve Regina " No. 4 (Moorat). " Tantum. Ergo " No. 7 (Murphy) and "0 Jesu Pester" (Winter). The soloists were the Misses Hakersley and Richardson, Messrs. J. Babbitt and J. M. Bate. The preacher was again the Rev. Father Jaa,gar, who based his remarks on a passage taken from St. Paul's Epistle to the Corinthians. Man, he said, seemed born to pain and suffering, and practical experience had shown that sorrow and suffering must necessarily be the lot of those who lived upon this earth. The question thenarose how were we to change the suffering into gold. It was not the primary intention of God that there should be universal suffering. Sorrow was the result of sin and was the direct penalty of sin. When we thought of the sorrow of Him whom we worshipped we SAW at once that there was a means of changing sorrow, of purging it of its dross and rendering it pure gold. By His crucifixion He had made it possible for us to change our sorrow by our love for Him. Only by taking our sorrows to Him and by coming with a loviagsimplicity before the cross 1 of Christ could we finci:thetrne consolatioaand balm for i the soul. -

The organ selections for the day, which were skilfully executed by Mr. Eckersley, inclnded "Andante con Moto in A" (Henry Smart), "Andante Grazioso " (Henry Smart), "Intermezzo and Scherzoso," "Sonata in E minor" (Rheinberger)," Sonata No I." two move- ments (Menletssohn). It may be stated that the organ was originally built by Messrs. Wadsworth Bros., of Manchester, by whom the work of renovation has been undertaken. It contains two complete manuals C.C. to G,and pedals C.C.C. to P. As a memento of the occasion Mr. Peter Bate, who renders most valuable service in training and conducting the choir, was presented by the mission clergy (Revs. Fathers McGrath and D'Arcy) with an ebony, gold mounted baton.

Wesley and Methodism.

This formed the subject of the address- which the Rev. H. Dannico delivered to a large congregation at the Parr Hall on Sunday eveniag. Great men, he said, were usually divided into two classes—men of thought and men of action—the former consisting of poets, philosophers, scientists and inventors, the latter of statesmen, reformers, and missionaries. John Wesley belonged to the latter; although it would not be difficult to find a place for him among the former. Wesley lived In an age of religion without morality, and of politics without honour and his success lay in the fact that he preached the love of God and embodied it in his life. Some people imagined that Wesley's groat power over the multitude was due to his painting lurid pictures of hall, but that was not so. He detested rant and cant as much as any- one. Religion to him was but another name for love. He was no bigot. He asserted that every good man was a child of God. He never sought to promote sectarian ends. thought very little of craeds, and imposed none on his followers: Methodists think- and let think," he said on one occasion. One condition, and one only, he required of his followers—a real desire to save souls. Wesley's outlook was as broad as humanity. He recog- nized that men had bodies as well as souls, and that Christianity must care for both. He had commonsense and invincible courage, and was willing to adopt any plan to uplift humanity. Modern social movements he anticipated by establishing an orphanage,a public school, a dispensary, a lending society, and the distribution of cheap literature. Money he cared nothing about, All he had he gave away.He died at the age of 83, without pain and went home. Mr. Dun nice pleaded for more of Wesley's spirit to-day. Differences of degree between Wesley and them there must be, but the spirit of his life might be theirs, and though one star differed from another in glory, they might all movein the same sphere and shine with the same radiance.

A SKELETON FOR THE KING.

The diplodocus, a prehistoric skeleton, of which there is no other specimen in Europe, has arrived in London. It is Mr. Carnegie's present to the King, and it will be built up in South Kensington Museum.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Epidemic of Measles.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Dig- trict Council was held at Bswsey Chambers on Wednes- day, Mr. T. Halton (presided) and there were also present Dr. Murray, Messrs. R. Forshaw, J. Backhouse, P. Longton, A. Brooks, W. Houghton, G. Mather, J, C. Sutton, (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor),ll. H. burnage (assistant surveyor) F. W. Kerfoot (inspector), and Dr. Sephton (medic. .1 officer).

RATES. The Council sanctioned the levying of a rate of sd. in the £ for general expenses.

THE HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. OUTBREAK OF MEASLES. The MEDICAL OFFICER reported that during the month there had bran 33 births and 41 deaths. The death rate was equal to 4202 per 1,000, as agairst 33.00 for the past three months and 41.7 for the correspond- ing period of last year. Twenty-eight of the deaths occured at the County Asylum, exclusive of which the death rate proper for tho district would be 13.3 per 1,000. The wastes epidemic at Surtonwood had spre^,cl throughout the district and upon his advice the St. Paul's schools had been closed for a fortnight. Hr. MATHER: Have you heard anything about Martinscroft ?

Dr. SEPITTON continuing said he had received a com- munication from a Priest at Martinscroft asking that the schools should be closed on account of an out-break of measles. He wkred to the inspector to inquire into the extent of the epidemic but the wire broke down and the inspector did not get the message until that morning. The inspector had, however, been over to Martiescroft that morning and had found that the number of scholars in daily attendance had gradually decreased until the schools had had to be closed. He (Dr. Sephton) recommended that the schools should remain closed for a fortnight. He had written to the local educatiou authority (Earlestown) with regard to the disinfecting o 1 the schools at Eiollins Green. Mr. Clarke had replied that the County Council did not think it necessary to have schools limewashed after infectious disease. Mr. Clarke apparently looked upon limewashing from a different point of view from him (the medical officer.) Mr. CLARKE thought it was to keep the place tidy, whereas it was simply for the purpose of preventing the spread of disease, and so long as he remained the medical officer to the Council he would try to protect the public by having schools disinfected and cleansed. (Hear, hear.) If the schools had been recently decorated he would suggest that the walls should be wiped down with a cloth damped with carbolic acid and water. The report was approved. TENDERS ACCEPTED.

The following tenders wore accepted :—Macadam, Messrs. Brundrett and Co., Limited, Darbyshires, Ltd., and Mr. Richard Allen ; setts, Messrs. J. Downs and Son, and Mr. J. Phillipson ; kerbs, Messrs.lWalton Bros. and Mr. J. Phillipson ; tiles, Mr. J. Dixon ; broken slag, Mr. Mcliechnie, Messrs. J. Tabberer ; spades and Sho,rels, Mr. J.E. Unsworth.

-COUNTY COUNCIL GRANTS. The SURVEYOR Mr. B. S. Newcombe), reported that the County Council had made special grants to the District Council of £470, for main roads, and £l4O for secondary roads.

VOTES OF THANKS. Mr. HOUGHTON proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Halton for his setvice- as Chairman during the year. Mr. Halton had, he said, carried out the duties of his office inn most .pable manner, and he thought it was a misfortune to the Council to have to lose his services. Although they could not, however, prevent his leaving them they could thank him for what he had done for the district ard for his (Mr. Halton's) parish. He had known Mr. Halton during the whole of his public career and if he had one fault it was that he was perhaps a little over zealous. Mr. FORSITAAV seconded. He admired Mr. Halton fcr the energy with which he had taken up t-,sks which had devoted time which any other man would have begrudged. Mr. MATHER expressed his regret that they were to lose Hr. Halton's servicca. He ht .rtily:endorsed all that had been said and was only sorry that Mr. Halton could not stop longer with them. Mr. Halton had been a very useful member of the Council.

The Vote was heartily accorded. Mr. HALTON said he regretted that he was leaving that happy Council. He had been connected with that body for ten years and during the whole of that time he had never had a wrong word %ith anyone. They had been a happy family during the period of his chairman- ship, and he hoped that the Council would continue as it had done in the plst. Although he would not be actively connectsd with them in the future he would still watch with interest the work of the council. He regretted that he had to sever his connection wita them. LALL Mears HESERVED.

MARBURG'S SECRET,

on THE THREE FOUNTAINS,

MARY LOVETT CAMERON (Author of" At a Moorish Window," 4c.)

One bright spring morning four young people left Rome by the San Paolo gate for a ramble on the Campagna. It was a day to tempt all lovers of nature from the contemplation of works of art, however perfect. A sapphire sky flecked here and there by little vagrant clouds arched over the sunlit landscape, and the buoyant stir of awaken- ing life was in the air. Walking first was Rosa- mund Hanleys, a slender English girl, whose firm light stop showed that she would be no clog on the progress of her male companions. Beside her was Andrew Scott, a young . artist, whose studio had been a favourite place of meeting for his friends during the past winter. Ile' kept his face turned towards his companion, and talked eagerly, watching the soft colour which the balmy air brought to her cheeks, and the quick um- pathetic play of her mobile features,

The fli.st Part of the way was flat and uninter- esting, but it seemed to fly beneath their feet, am they covered it, laughing and chatting. When they came to the turning in the road where Stn Paolo flings its vast Mass athwart the prospect,/ Rosamund paused and looked back. "We have outwalked the others," she said. Scott drew his level brows together, and tapped the ground impatiently with him foot. •

"We need not wait. Edgar and Marburg are deep in one of their archteological discussions, I expect." The others utile nearer. Edgar Harnley, who was at least ten years older than his mister, bore the mark of a scholar on his slightly bowed shoulders, and in his tired eyes; his hands were clasped behind his back, and he looked down as he spoke in serious measured tones, to which, not- withstanding Scott's surmise, his companion hardly seemed to be giving his attention. The tallest and by far the handsomest of the Tarty, all of whom were comely, Gottfried Marburg, was a south German of the untleshly type, which the early painters and engravers of his nation were fond of taking for their soldier saints and hermit knights. A tinge of melancholy shadowed his clear cut feazures and his blue eyes, fringed by long lashes, had the strange limpid gaze of a young. child.

„ ” Which way shall we take ?" asked Rosa- mund. "We have made the round by the Sette Cldesi and S. Domitilla so often, let us go to Tri lontane to-day," said Edgar. All were agreed and they set forth again. As long as they followed the highway beyond the basilica they kept together, but when they were mounting the steep lane beyond the little lies- telry, which marks the hist vestige of life on the lonely road, Rosamund stopped to gather some I thyme from the high overhanging bank. Ilsr- burg waited for her. Andrew Scott 'gave a back- ward glance, but Edgar was in the midst of a description of some newly discovered frescoes, and he bad no excuse for interrupting hint. Gott- fried did not any anything at first, but wltked in silence beside Rosamund till they reached the top of the bill, where the lane, disengaging itself from the high banks, runs along the lop of a ridge commanding the desolate expanse of the Campagna. Not a house, not a hedge row, not a ' cultivated field is to be seen. The few miserable huts of the fever-stricken inhabitants of this deadly district, are built into the strangely shaped ruins, which, seeming to have crumbled into the very substance of the ground, are hardly to be distinguished from them.

_ "And this was once a busy, •fasbionable sub- urb," said Rosamund, " i 1 seems incredible

Dust to dust," murmured Marburg, tnore tc himself than in answer to her remark. " There is such infinite sadness in a SCMIO onee cultivated rind lived in by men, then abati- ciou;d." she went on, " so different from the joy- ful wildness of nature, left to herself."

" There is a beauty—but it is the beauty of old age mid death." _ _ _

Just then a bend in the road brought a grove of thickly foliaged trees in sight : they filled a hollow in the ground, and stretched up over the hillside beyond. Tiled roofs and a little dome and belfry peeped out among them. Edgar stopped and pointed with his stick. "There are the groves of eucalyptus by which the monks of the Abbey of Tri Fontane are exor- cising the demon of malaria. Thirty years ago to dwell here meant death, the monastery was abandoned, the churches in ruins. Then a few Trappist monks, grieved that the reputed scene of St. Paul's martyrdom should be thus deserted, volunteered to try to reclaim the desert. They planted and drained, and though it is hardly yet % sanatorium, the unhealthiness is supportable to resolute and vigorous men." They descended the slope and following an avenue of eucalyptus, came to a gothic archway, at the gate of which they rang. It swung open, and a few steps brought them into an open court round which the churches and other buildings of the monastery were grouped. The change of scene was as sudden as complete. Outside the gate all was dreary desolation. Within masses of flowers, and banks of blooming shrubs sur- rounded a plashing fountain from the stone basin in which trailed verdant creepers ; a puppy and a kitten played in the sunlight, and the soft cooing of doves mingled with the chant rolling forth from the cool gloom of the open church-door. An old monk clad in white woollen garments came forward cautiously, and led them by a flower bordered path to the little chapel of the three fountains which gives its name to the place. Rosamund looked at Gottfried Marburg: the sadness had left his face. " The desert lives again," she said.

, " 'Tie nature only waiting to be reconquered," and he smiled a strange pathetic smile, which tilled Rosamund with , wondering pity. They wandered about, plunging their faces in the sweet smelling bunches of flowers, sitting on the steps leading to, the churches, and on-the margins of the bubbling fountains. At last Edgar warned them that it was time to depart. They had a • long stretch of the most un- healthy pert of the Campagna to traverse, and the sun was getting low. When they came to the gate they Raw that Marburg was no longer with them : they called him, but in vain. Edgar would not let them wait longer, he asked the porter to tell their friend they had gone on, and they set off on their way towards Rome. They reached the gates just at sundown but Gottfried Mar- burg had not overtaken them. Late that evening; as Andrew Scott sat reading 111 his studio. Marburg walked in. " I have come to wish you good-bye," he be- gan abruptly, "I am leaving to-morrow morn- ing." " This is a sudden idea, isn't it ? I hope no bad news—"

"Quite the contrary," replied Marburg, and he smiled the odd far-away smile that had puzzled Rosamund. " I have known for some time that I must go, that I should be summoned—but I have lingered." _ _ _

He took a turn up and down the studio. An- drew watched him, letting his pipe go out in his surprise. Presently Marburg came and stood before him. "I have had many pleasant hours here—they have been .the breathing times in a struggle that has been very hard sometimes—but, never mind, it is over now. And so good-bye and thank you." "Confound it all, Marburg, you talk as if you were going to die." Gottfried was feeling in his pocket. "I have one last little favour I would ask you to do for me." He brought out a letter. " Will you give this to Miss Hanley P" "Surely you are going to wish them good- bye !" "No3—better not; it is late to-night, and lam off at daybreak to-morrow. I could post this of course—but—you will explain—in fact I should like you to give it to her." "Of course I'll give it to her if you ask me," said Andrew, taking the note and thrusting it in his pocket. "Bat its a pity you can't put off going a day, and wish the tlauley's goodbye; after being together so much—it seems strange.' "My note explains—they will understand. And now I must be off, I have still much to do to-night. Goodbye:•• He held out his nand. Andrew grasped it without further speech and Marburg departed. When he wits gone Andrew shook his fist at the door behind which .he.had disappeared.

_ "I wonder whether you suspect —you cold- blooded Teuton," ho muttered between his teeth, "To make me your go-between—your letter carrier !" lie drew several violent puffs at his pipe. "Is be doing it to choice mo off or to put me on my honour not to cat him out while L'e is away What on earth does the :fellow mean i"

The next morning found Andrew enquiring for the Matleys of the porter nt the old house on the Janieninnt where they had In ken an apart- ment for the winter. The Signor llnnley had al- ready gone out, he wus told, but the Signorina was within. Andrew went tip. lin looked pale 41dcl. haagard. no if he bad nut slept ell nUtt ;

when he saw Ensatninat lie grew paler ana seemed to find a difficulty in speaking. "Edgar has gone to the library in the Colligie Romano," she began—" but you don't look well, is anything wrong ?" " No—yes—iu fact Marburg is gone." " Gone 1"

" Yes—gone, left Rome, that is to say." " Without wishing us good-bye. 011 ! some- thing dreadful must have happened. Tell me quick." " I can't. He said he was summoned. He was mystorious, but he left a letter to you which explaXis all about it." " A letter. Where is it ?" a All That's just it. he gave it to me to give you—and I—have7lost it."

- - • Andrew was red now, furiously red to the very rots of his : he looked up, as be spoke, and met Rosamund's eyes, and his heart sickened. for be saw a suspicion flash into them. They stood facing each other a few seconds, then Rosa- mund, ii a voice she tried to make indifferent, said, "1 daresay there was nothing important in it, he will come back and we shall know all about And she sat down, and took some work from the fable. From that day the friends fell apart; the iambics on the Campagna, the wanderings about the ruins, and through the museums, which had wiled away so pleasantly their leisure hours were all over ; so were the chats round Rosamund's tea-table in the twilight, when they discussed what they bad 'seen and done during the day, from veried but sympathetic -points of view. Andrew Scott shut himself up in his studio, Edgar Hanley buried himself more than ever in his books, Rosamund grew pale and sil- ent. Of Marburg no word was heard. - Spring merged into summer, and the time came for the Hanleys to leave Rome for London, where Edgar was going to see his book on Roman Monuments of the Decadence through the press. A few days before their departure .Edgar came in to tea bringing with him Scott. " I have been to -look this fellow up at his studio, and insisted on his coining hack with me. We have seen nothing of him lately, he is over- working. Give him a cup of tea, Rosy." Conversation flagged: both Andrew and Rosa- mund looked unhappy, and talked in jerks. Edgar was not quick-sighted, except when en ancient inscription was in question, but he felt all was not right. "We aro not so jolly AR we used to be. I think we miss Marburg. By the way, has any- thing been beard of him, Scott , Andrew looked into his cup. " Not a word," he said, then lifting his head and looking full at Rosamund he added, " But I have made up my mind to find him. I shall leave no 'stone untamed to get on his track." Her face lighted up. "Ohdo ! Find him for as,!" she cried. He jumped up, her frank expression of joy stung hint.

" I must say good-bye, Hanley. You return to Rome in the autumn, do you not r " Assuredly. My book on Etrurian remains on the Campagna will bring' me. And you Are you staying on here " Artists don't flee Rome in summer. It is the finest time here for work. I may go up to the Alban hills for a few weeks, but no further." " Then we meet again in November," said Edgar, as Andrew shook bands vtli Rosamund. She smiled as cordially as ever, but he went away with a heavy heart : whether he succeeded or failed in his quest, he saw no •hope of obtain- ing_his heart's desire.

„ November came. The Tianleys returned to Rome. An aunt and some cousins came with them for a short stay, and they were busy at first showing them round. Andrew called, but he could not get an opportunity of speaking to Rosamund apart. He just managed to say as she passed him a tea-cup. ” have not found Marburg." When he was leaving fillo mid.= "We are driving out on the Appiaii Way to- morrow, taking our lunch, and afterwards aunt will drive back with the baskets, and the rest of the party mean to walk to the Latin Tombs and home by the Porta Sun Giovanni. Will you go with us?"

Her eyes said " accept," and he aceep.ted.

, The itext day, lunch being over, and tbe aunt having driven oil in the carriage, Edgar began to point out and explain some of the most inter- esting tombs to his cousins. This was the oppor- tunity Rosamund bad foreseen. She turned off the road, and after going a few steps sat down behind one of the strangely shaped masses of ruin which are all that remain of the tombs of so many noble Romans. Andrew had followed her, and they sat side by side a few minutes without speaking. It was a wind-still autumn day; before them like the rolling billows of the sea, the Campagna swept away ; near at hand, sparsely clothed by brownish grass, it melted gradually into indes- cribable shades of sad soft colour till at last it wrapped itself around the feet of the distant pur- ple bills. Not a sound, not a movement broke the silence ; all nature seemed to pause in grey suspense. • If " Marburg has disappeared, I said Andrew presently. " Disappeared as utterly as if the ground had swallowed him up." He waited a moment, and as Rosamund said nothing, went on. " I have been able to find no clue. Ho left no address at his hotel, we were his only friends in Rothe. There was always something strange about him. With all his charm, and his sym- pathetic interest in my work, my hopes and plans, he was reserved ; he never spoke of him- self." "Ha had had a great sorrow, and had been obliged to break with all his past. lam not surf even that Marburg was his name." " He had confided in you I" "Only in general terms; stray words, momen- tary self-betrayals told me all I know ; I think it. was something he could not bear to tell. I imagine (not that he said so) that the sin of another had fallen on him. He was very, very lonely." Andrew struck the turf beside him with his clenched fist. All the hopes that had sprung up in the long summer days vanished. Marburg, with his godlike face, and his tragic secret, was a rival whose power over a girl's imagination must be supreme. His pain made him bitter and un j ust. "And knowing all this you encouraged me in a futile search, a search that was bound to fail 1" "I wanted you to try," she said. " That letter, that damned letter i• "It might have given us the key—but on the other hand it might only have been to say good- bye for ever." Andrew suppressed the words that rose to his lips. He wanted to cry out, to tell her not to waste her youth in hoping against hope, in wait- ing for one who had disappeared for ever from her life. But how amid he do so, when the letter entrusted to him perchance explained all that was mysterious in Marburg's conduct ? How if in it he had craved patience, asked her to await his return freed from some suspicion or misfortune? And if that were so, mid Marburg did come back, he, Andrew, would stand for ever accused of hav- ing destroyed the missive. Ho sprang up. The tension was too severe for his impulsive, passion- ate nature. To be so near her, to feel the burning words quivering oil his tongue, and mint dare to say them, was beyond his slrength. The distant hills, the weird Campagna, the ruined tombs all mine before his eyes. • " I think walk straight home. Excuse me to the others. I don't feel well. I have a lot of things to do." Rosamund looked np at him, but be was gaz- ing straight in front of hint, ntid did not ace the appeal in her eyes. " I have done 111, Ti."" v" me. von ,'an- mint tO.,111) bin digeovery more than L du. If eduitl find him he would be here." Pouring out his words confusedly, ho shook hands, and was striding away over the brown earth and scanty grass, before Rosamund could find a word to bid him stay.

When he was fairly gone she let her face fall on her bands and tears squeezed themselves be- tween her fingers.

" It is all my fault —my tricked, horrid sus- picion that morning, when he told me the note was lost. It as only for a minute, but he saw it. ,'JIo saw it, and he is so proud and head- strong —I love 1 • for it—bat ho is. He will never forgive me, unless he could bring me the etter and fling it down before me, or find Mar- burg. And Gottfried, poor fellow ! how grieved ha would be if he knew that he had come be- tween. us !" Quitting by this train of thought her own troubles, she went on to wonder for the huu- dri•dth time ' what had become of her friend. Friend, she could truly call him, for something intangible but very real, had from the first mad; impossible any other feeling between them. Ho tvits cut off from the common lot of man. Con- demned to stand aloof and watch the joys and sorrows of others. What the summons could be of which he had spoken to Andrew she could not guess.. _

Ilittsdng time Rho sat on 'Where Andrew had left Jr, till it suddenly oe,curred to her that the others would. expect .to meet her with Scott at the Latin tombs. She hastened back to the rowl aml began to walk along it hoping that she hail not forgotten which turning to take to get to the

Latin Way. The Appian Way stretched its rib- bon-like length between its rows of tombs: there was not a creature in sight. Now she perceived that the western horizon was burnishing as if with the glow of sunset : the November days are short, and she must have sat much longer than she had dreamt, after Andrew bad left her. After a while, anxious thoughts began to assail her, for the odd-shaped ridges which futrow the Campagna hid the fow of tombs whose ir- regular forms mark the course of the Appian Way She hoped she was walking parallel with it, but she could not be sure. flowerer, it was useless to try to retrace her steps ; in her first ter- rified flight she had not noticed where she went ; the best plan now was to walk on, and trust to either striking the road or coating on some habi- tation where she could get a guide to lead her back to the gates of Rome. The gloom deep- ened, the light of day was almost gone, the first star twinkled in time sky, and still she saw no sign of human life. just as /Ilia was contem- plating the prospect of spending the night in solitary wandering she saw a light, her heart, leaped, and she hurried towards it. As she op, preached it she found a low bank or wall of loose stones in her way. Skirting it for a while and finding no gate, she decided to scramble over, but hardly had she gained the top when a huge dog, which must have been crouching on the other side,. sprang at her, and she started buck, and the great animal stood on the wall, his fangs exposed, giving vent to a menacing snarl. How log she stood thus not daring to move, lest the brute should seize her, she did not know, —pro- Itably only a few moments, but they seemed lung to her. Suddenly something whirled past her, IL long staff wielded by a stalwart arm swept the top of the wall, the dmg disappeared with ii yelp, and turning she beheld white-robed monk beside her. lie beckoned, and they walked quialy off while the dog was recovering, from his surprise. They heard his barks begin after it few moments, but content to guard his master's properly, he did not venture out, and they be- , mime fainter and fainter in the distance. •

She turned to her cootp-finioti, his cowl CNA drawn forward, Rua jut lie (husk his face was quite invisible.
