Meanwhile Andrew bad gone on his way caring little Whither his feet carried Lim, so lmig as he walked oil the violence of his feelings by rapid motion. He had struck on! across country, and he also found himself (though much earlier in the afternoon) in the neighbourhood of the ['re Pen- tane. By that time his excitement had somewhat cooled, and entering tie wood on the bill above the monastery be sat down to rest. He began to regret his fit of temper' end to see the rudeness of his behaviour clearly. He was struck with com- punction for thus leaving- Rosamund to return to the party alone, and to wonder what excuse ho could offer when be saw Edgar again. His pipe was a counsellor be always had recourse to in his difficulties, and be felt for it in the side pocket of his coat. Then occurred one of those little acci- dents, which sometimes so diSproportionately af- fect the course of our lives. His pocket wits ragged (artist-like he loved old garments) and his pipe had slipped through a rent and into the lining of the coat. His -present humour was not one to bear thwarting patiently, he dragged the pocket inside out, and tore the hole much larger. Then he put his band in and felt for the pipe, but the first thing be got out was it crumpled en- velope, which be threw on the ground, but when be had extracted_ and lit the pipe he picked it up and examined it. The outside was so discoloured anddirty that be could not read the address: it was unopened, and he concluded that it must be one be had put in his pocket to post, and then for- gotten. He tore it open, and began to read : " When you receive this I shall have taken the step that will cut me off for ever front a world itt which a useful life is, through another's crime, made impossible to me. To-day, as we stood in that garden made to blossom in the desert, as I saw how health was begotten from disease, life froon death, by nameless men forgotten by the world, my vocation was suddenly made clear to me. lam sending this letter by the hand ut Scott—"

The paper was • crushed in Audrew's excited grasp. " Harrah ! Hurrah I have it. Found ut last !"

Then he ran over the situation in his mind. All was /made clear; while he had been weary- ing himself in fruitless search, Marburg was quietly dwelling in the building whose roof bc could see between the trunks of the eucalyptus trees below him. How strange" that lie should make this discovery just here ! And following an irresistible impulse he strode down the hill end rang at the gate of the Abbey. He inquired for Marburg, but the porter said he knew none of the inmates by their mundane names. How- ever, lie summoned the old monk whose office it was to receive visitors, an from him Andrew soon learnt that Gottfried was indeed at Tre Fontane. He had not yet taken religious vows. the Order was not entered until after long rroba- tiou, but he was living their life and wearing their habit, testing his capacity to sustain the severity of their discipline. Andrew asked if be might see him. "I have something of importance to tell him." The monk said there would be no objection, but brother Alessio, as they called him, was out at the moment. Ho Lad gone to carry aid to a shepherd who had met with an accident ; it was some tance away on the Campagna, but be expected be would soon be back. The bell rang while the monk was speaking. "This is probably brother Alessio, it is too late for visitors." -

Andrew bid a hasty farewell to the old roalb and went to the entrance : he found the porter in the act of opening the gate. Puffin°. hint asida h° stepped out holding the letter in his hand. BY the light of a lamp banging under the archwaY be distinguished two figures, and stopped a 0" tounded as he recognised Rosamund. Then Gottfried Marburg threw back the cowl which hid his face, and taking a hand of each of his two friends in one of his own he looked from one to the other with a smile of indescribable peace and tenderness.

" God bless yoa. God bless you both," b.e said, and joining their bands as be withdrew lo; own he turned from them, and disappoire'; through the open gate into the quiet courtyard

SCIENCE NOTES AND

GLEANINGS.

SEX AND TASTE. M. Vaschide has proved that man is more sen- sitive than woman to a salt taste, and in less degree to a bitter taste, while the two sexes are about equally sensitive to acid and sweet tastes. With a better developed olfactory sense, however, Woman is better able to distinguish flavours.

k AID. FOR LUNG DISEASES

A German has been studying the effects of Pyrenol, which is a combination of salicylic acid, benzoic acid, and thymol in the form of a sodium salt. It was found useful in asthma, whooping Cough, pneumonia, bronchitis, rheumatic affec- tions, pleurisy, tonsillitis, and gout.. It has been found specially valuable for the dry, irritating tough of consumption.

LEAST HARMFUL LIGHT FOR THE EYES. The injurious effects of light •on the eyes are found to increase with the number of ultra-violet or chemical rays contained. This is the conclusion 0 Dr. A. Staerkle, of Basel, who shews that Petroleum is the least harmful light, and is followed In order by gaslight, electric light, incandescent gaslight, and acetylene, the last-named being most harmful. Thick coloured glass—gray-yellow, red or green—lessens the injurious rays.

THE SNAKE'S TONGUE. The snake's tongue proves to be a most remark- able organ. A Marylard woman student finds that its chief function is connected with a sense of feeling without touch, and may be a finer develop- ment of the sense that enables some people to avoid striking obstacles in the dark. The forked tip and tile numerous folds behind it greatly increase the surface exposure. The cells of the epidermis are interlaced by a network of extremely fine, nerve bres, which centre in a deep ivrve plexus beneath the epirderuis and extending one into the folds.

THE WEIGHT OF A CROWD. Professor Johnson, an American, and Herr Hun- ocheidt, architect to the German Government, hava both found that the weight of a crowd may be as nluell as 600 or more kilogrammes per square metre. Flunscheidt's average is 625 kilogrammes, ?tith a minimum of 525 and a maximum of 706 galogrammes a square metre. Johnson got a "axlmum of 750 kilogrammes for that area. No Qoubt the figure will vary somewhat according to the nation and race, as well as the press of the ,eroWd. Erections to bear crowds should be .."45igned according to these results.

WONDERS OF ATOiNIS. • Professor J. J. Thomson, some years ago, pro- evidence of the existence of negatively elec- 'rifled Particles much smaller than atoms, having a Mass equal in round numbers to about one- thousandth that of the hydrogen atom. A very', B,l,lggestive fact concerning these particles, says "r•492neering, was that they appeared to be identical, Whatever the source from which they were derived. however derived, they appeared to be always the isanie---a fact which, in conjunction with their small !tze,suggested that every atom might be constituted 03' a group of these corpuscles.

QUICK WINKS. The closing of the eye, the rest, and the opening af it, have been measured, and are found to con. surse 395,000ths of a second. This, says Health, is a quick wink, and winks have been measured that required 10,000ths of a second more time. For these measurements an especially arranged photo- apparatus is required, and then, when a of white paper is placed on the eyelid, its .adtions can be accurately determined. In a quick *hilt the closing of the eye has been found to tequire 75,000ths of a second, the rest twice as long, and the opening 170,000ths of a second.

The • - Pigment is always there—just sufficient in strength to resist the danger in different climes. This accounts for the varying colours of different 1 races—black, red, yellow, copper, and white. XXXIL Screw and Reverse Rotation

(CONTINUED.)

Many ordinary players nearly always use side when playing screw strokes, but although side is generally advisable and often absolutely neces- sary when playing screw in-offs, the ordinary, player should entirely discard it when playing ball-to-ball screw cannons with the second object ball well away from a cushion. Really good players often use sido when screwing from one ball on to another but the side is employed in. order to leave good after position, and not as an auxiliary in the actual makiig of the cannon. For example screw cannons with the cue ball near the first object ball, can be made with con- siderably less strength if played 'with running side, than if check side be used and these slow, screws will be discussed in a later article..

--The- ordinary player who has not mastered gentle-strength screws and who in fact plays all screws at a high pace should strike his ball without side when playing ball-to-ball screw cannons for more reverse rotation can be im- parted to a ball by hitting it low down on its central lino than by hitting it towards the side even though low down. "Side helps the screw", is an old saying, and yet though as alreadyl stated, running side may often be advan- tageously employed when the cue ball is near the object ball, generally Speaking side by lessen- ing the reverse rotation weakens the screw. Especialy is this the case when the cue ball is a long way from the first object ball. To many ordinary players the screw in-off from the red on the spot is a big screw and generally beyond their power, and yet often enough they weaken the screw by playing the stroke with running side under the erroneous impression .that the side will assist the screw. Screw in-offs differ from screw cannons in that they shotild (except when the cue ball is a long way from the object ball and the pocket is moderately open) almost always be played with side. Generally check side has to be used but sometimes the correct side is running side. In many positions the in-off would be virtually impossible without check side. In other cases although the in-off could be made without the use of any side at all, check side 'makes the stroke far more certain. Often also the in-off can be made easily enough, with çither running side or check side, but running side must be used if good position is to be retained. Many players are thus often at a difficulty in determining which side to use when playing certain screw in-offs, but though it is manifestly impossible to lay down a hard and \ fast rule which will cover all positions, for the simple reason that by small gradations one position can be made to insensibly merge into another, the 'following may be taken as a sound axiom:— When the pocket is a• blind one chfck side must be used, but when the pocket is a very open one and the balls are near each other, running side should be used. It may be asked what consti- tutes a blind pocket, and though here aggin no hard and fast rule can be laid down, I fvill in this chapter on SCREW constantly refer to blind and open Pockets.

Two screw in-offs.—Position A, red 13/4 inL the top cushion and 16 inches from the side cushion. Cue ball 9 inches from the top cushion. and 131 inches from the side cushion. ball, the reverse rotation tends to cause the ball to travel in the direction it is rotating. When an object ball has been struck anything less than' full, it is impossible for the cue ball to travel in the direction of its reverse rotation. The com- promise is a more or less pronounced curve, with its inner or concave side towards the striker. Most players are aware of the curve that tho cue balt describes in all half-ball forcing strokes.. In this case, however, 'the- curve is convex to , the striker owing to the ball being under the influence of forward rotation. The convex curve of the cue ball in forcing strokes is generally far more noticeable than the concave curve in screw strokes, but this is _because forward rota- tion does not become exhausted nearly so quickly as reverse rotation._

RHEUMATISM AND ECZEMA. Astonishing Zam-Buk Cure.

Eczema associated with rheumatism, or in some way arising out of rheumatism, is not at all an uncommon complaint. Such a case has just been under observa- tion at Nottingham, and the fact of Zam-Buk having effected its cure renders it of value and interest to local readers. _

The patients was Mr. Henry Savage, miner, of 57, Brassey-street, Alfreton-road,Nottingriam. "For about eight years," says he, "I have been on and off my club owing to this sickness. My skin was in a terrible state, particularly about the left leg. It was one mass of sores from knee to ankle, and though, of course, I kept it swathed in bandages and did all I. could to alleviate my sufferings, the agony, was fearful. Thepain was very bad, and I often wished I were dead and out of it. I had he greatest difficulty in getting about. I had difficulty in getting my heel to the ground, and I often had to ride where before I could walk. You cannot imagine the state of my leg. Fancy being laid up four months at a stretch! Even to-day it frightens me to think of the plight I was in, and -of the torture I have gone through these many years. I had treatment both privately and at the hospital. The doctor said some- thing about it being due to rheumatism in the bone, and gave me some lotions to use These did not appear to give me relief, however ; and one day, later on, when he saw that I had not dressed my leg with his stuff but with something else, he asked what it was. My wife said 'lt's Zam-Buk he's on with, and it's done him a great deal of good already.' He appeared sceptical, but when I told him I was actually feeling better, he added, Well, then, go on trying it if you like.' So you see the doctor was no prepared to deny the value of Zam- Buk. I had heard good accounts of its success before and this bad made me give it a trial. I persevered with Zam-Buk, and although I had just been ten weeks off work while under other treatment, I was fit to start again within a fortnight after beginning to use Zam- Buk. Zam-Buk did more for me than the hospital and doctors put together. It gave me ease and relief such as I had never enjoyed for many a year. Instead of tossing about the pillow as I used to, I can now get a quiet night's rest as a regular thing. My skin is healthy, the irritation has disappeared, and I can walk about quite comfortably. I can walk ten times better than I cquld seven weeks ago. There's no doubt about it,Zam- Bilk has been worth its weight in gold to me. I hope others may benefit like I have done," Zam-Buk expels poison from the system, and pro- duces, new healthy skin. For eczema, psoriasis, ulcers, pimples, ringworms, sore heads, sore backs, bad legs, piles, abscesses, boils, running soresoore breasts,chafing, stiffness, sprains, cuts, bruises burns, scalds, poisoned wounds, festering sores, rheumatism, neuralgia, sciatica, Zam-Buk has no equal. The stroke at A is consequently considerably easier than the one at B, for in position A, the side, act- ing in conjunction with the nap of the cloth as explained in the chapter on Side), tends to pull the cue ball to the side cushion in case it is not running quite close to it, whereas' in position B the side tends to pull the ball away from the cushion owing to its travelling right against the nap. This effect of the nen means a great deal in such a stroke, for in the one ease its influence will often cause the pocket to be found when the stroke has not been as at..eurately played as it might have been, and in the other it will as often be the cause of the stroke failing. There was no half-heartedness about the way War- rington avenged the crest-fallen and unfortunate Leighites. They reproduced much ot the form which carried them into the final a year ago, and the visitors, to their great surprise, were never in the hunt. Wigan proved adept at getting possession in the scram, but when it came to downright pushing, and open field work, they were out-classed. Play started in a very lively fashion and both lines had narrow escapes. Only ten minutes had elapsed when the scoring opened in sensational style. Brooks, Fish, and Preston rushed the Wigan full-back from a short kick and " Mr. Safety-Pin" Mason failing to hold, Preston got F)ssession at the quarter-line and ran over unopposed, to the mighty cheers of the War- rington supporters. Of course the point was lucky, but it came as a result of smart footwork and fast following-up. The men were there when the chanc - presented itself and so they ought to be! The scorer added a neat goal, and the locals were on very goDd terms with themselves.

The Late Mr. R W. Murray. Sir,—You reproduced in las t week's issue some remarks of " The Christian World" touching the late Mr. R. W. Murray. I had noted those remarks, and had written to that paper a letter of which I enclose you a copy. In this week's issue, he editor of the "Christian World" inserts some extracts from my letter and suppresses others. As there are many in Warring- ton who remember 2.lr.Murray with respect and esttern, you may like to reproduce for their perusal the letter I sent to the" Christian World."—Truly yours, JOHN YONGE.

April 7th. 1911. rTo THE EDITOR OF THE "CHRISTIAN WORLD.") Sir,—l have read with feelings of regret the para- graph in your last week's issue touching my old and esteemed friend, the late R. W. Murray, of Belfast. It is now many years since he transferred his residence to Ireland, and save very occasionally I have hardly seen him. But I think if I had composed the paragraph in question I could easily, and with more accuracy and justice, have presented a far more favourable testimony. In his Warrington days, he was a devoted adherent of the Church of which I was, and still am, the Pastor. He discharged his duties as worshipper, member, deacon. and Sunday school superintendent with a regularity and fidelity rarely equalled, and never excelled. He was gener- ally and widely respected and beloved, and by none more than the scholars and teachers who rejoiced in him as their leader. His removal was regarded with unanimous regret, and he left in the possession of the utmost confidence and affection. Subsequently he severed his connection with Liberalism, expousing the Unionist policy,and cherished I fear,a good deal of unhappy feeling about old political friends. That perhaps resulted in some degree of alienation on both sides, and tended, I think, to cast a shadow on his heart and life. But I greatly deprecated the possibility of his memory being held in lessened esteem by what I cannot regard otherwise then as a needless and considerably inaccurate' criticism of his later days. It should, I think, be re- membered that although the deadare beyond the reach either of our praise or its opposite, the living are sensitive. I am confident that those who knew Mr. Murray here or in Ireland would testify that there were many good things of him which could with greater pleasantness and yet greater truth have been recorded. Yours faithfully. JOHN YONDE.

When Preston found himself with a clear course to the line he seemed almost ashamed to take it, and looked round for the refesee's permission. Meanwhile D. Davies and others were up and frantic shouts of "go on Jack" decided the question and the lengthy forward legged it to the line at his best place. Had he dashed off instanter he could have rounded the posts. It pays to "play the whistle," and forwards especially should bear the fact in mind. If they score first and leave the legality of the decision to the referee they will not be far wrong.

The reverse to the Wiganers seemed to unnerve them somewhat. Mason was to be sympathised with, and his play for the next half hour showed that his confidencelhad received a shock. Warrington gave their opponents no quarter, and both• fore and aft the work of the team was excellent. They were continu- ally aggressive, and both wings needed a lot of watching. Fish performed wonders, while Kenyon was little behind him, and got his reward first. At hot attack just before the interval left Dickinson in possession, and passing at the right moment to Kenyon, the latter dashed over at the corner at a great pace. Warrington thus lead by 8 points on changing ends.

There was now little hope among the Wiganers of stemming the tide, while the Warrington men were correspondingly confident. They continued to overplay the visitors by superior combination and ware soon credited with another 5 points. Isherwood made one of those openings for Fish which be alone is capable of, and the left-winger completed the effort in finished style, himself adding the goal from a wide angle. Wigan had rather more of the game after this simply because Warrington eased the pressure. They attacked!! frequently, but never seemed likely to circumvent the smarter men opposed to them. Leytham was practically a spectatoz, which proves how effectual were the breaking-up methods of the home halves and centres. The closing stages were rather uneventful. and Warrington ran out easy winners by 2 goals, 3 tries (13 points) to nil.

THE REV. JOHN GUNNELL'S RETIREMENT. Present of 100 Guineas. After fifteen year's devoted service in the Warrington Wesleyan Circuit the Rev. John Gunnell, who has been more particularly identified with the work at Penketh, is retiring from active circuit life. By the people amongst whom he has laboured he is held in the warmest affection and respect, and striking testimony to his worth as a minister and friend was forthcoming on Thurzday evening when the rev, gentleman was the recipient of a present of a cheque for a hundred guineas, and an illuminated address from the Wesleyans of the circuit. The pre- sentation was made by Miss Bessie Dutton at a largely- attended meeting held in Bold-street schoolroom, and Presided over by Mr. J. G. Withinshaw (senior circuit steward). The CHAIRMAN SA id that last September Mr. Gunnell intimated that he must cease his official connection with the circuit, but they were pleased to say they had him unofficially still. (Applause.) the Last September he completed fifteen years in circuit--three years as superintendent and twelve yearsl as supernumerary at Penketh. The longer Mr. Gunnell stayed amongst them, the fonder they became of him. Wherever one went in the circuit, Mr. Gunnell was spoken of with the deepest affection. Very few men could equal him in any way. He (the chair- man) had never heard him repeat a bit of scandal or say an unkind or unjust thing of anyone. Mr. Gunnell had been his ideal of a Christian gentleman in every sense ithe word. He was, moreover, a fearless man and Always said what he considered right. At Penketh he had been untiring in his work and they all hoped that both Mr. and Mrs. Gunnell would long be spared to stay amongst them. (Applause.) ' Mr. J. Upsos (junior circuit steward) also bore testi- mony to the warmth and depth of the affection which they all felt for Mr. Gunnell. It was his inherent worth which appealed strongly to them—the beauty of his character, the splendid pulpit services ho had rendered, the way in which he had gone into their homes and touched them in their domestic lives, and the wonderful mellowing which they had noticed in his character. While acting in the wisest way in the interests of Methodism and of Christ's Church,he had: always shown a tender regard for those with whom he had come into contact. Mr. W. SILCOCK (circuit secretary) aided his testi- mony to Mr. Gunnell's worth and said the highest encomium they could bestow upon him was to say that he lived in the hearts of his people. When the history of that Church came to be written not least among the able men who had ministered in the circuit would be found the name of the Rev. John Gunnell. Mr. William Bennett (Paigate), Mr. G. Weaver (Bold- street), Mr. J. W. Hayward (Bewsey-road), Mr. R. G. Henshall (Latchford), and Mr. W. Hickham (on behalf of the local preachers) also made complimentary

There was plenty of poetic justice about the result. Wigan did not deserve to be in the Competition after securing a place through a protest, and they were effectually put out. Then on February 18 they heavily defeated 'Warrington, and this, too, was atoned for. Warrington's three matches with Wigan this season have resulted as follows : At Warrington, 9-0, and 12-0 ; at Wigan 8-10. Preston has been a prominent factor in the two victories. In the first erga7ement he scored all the nine points, while on Saturday he obtained the first five. He is one of the finest for- wards who ever played in the Warrington pack.

I have already indicated that Warrington produced their best form in this match. The forwards, though beaten for possession frequently, were by far the stronger and more dashing set. The Warrington backs quite outclassed the Wiganers, and their aggres- sive, go-ahead tactics raised the enthusiasm of the crowd to a high pitch. It is almost impossible to single any of them out. Perhaps the least famous deserve the most notice. Brooks is making a grand substitute for Hockenhall, and with practice seems likely to train on into a classy half of the robust order. Kenyon on the right wing improves weekly, and his speed and dash arc making an effective counter- balance to the brilliance of Fish on the left. Warring- ton have got a side together which encourages the highest hopes.

Warrington will have to visit the Athletic Grounds at Rochdale to play off the semi-final tie. On Wednes- day Bradford successfully accounted for Oldham and thus qualify as Warrington's opponents in the semi- final for the second time. Last season Warrington and Bradford drew on the Broughton Rangers ground the replay at Huddersfield being won in magnificent style by Warrington. The task was then, and is now a severe one, for Bradford are looked upon as the strongest team in the Northern Union. There is plenty of confidence in the Warrington men, however. They have a knack of excelling themselves against Bradford,as witness the two engagements this season. Warrington won handsomely at home and only lost by a goal-kick at Park Avenue.

speeches. Miss BI,NSIE Drrrox said she esteemed it one of the greatest honours and privileges of her life to make the presentation. She particularly wished to testify to the great love they all had for Mrs. Gunnell. (Applause.) The present was a mere nothing compared with what the Wesleyans in the circuit had received from Mr. and Mrs. Gunnell. They had lived the true, consistent lives of a Christian lady and gentleman, (Applause.) Miss Dutton handed the present to Mr. Gunnell amid applause. The Rev. Joins: GUNNELL, in returning thanks, said he was afraid they had formed a very excessive estimate of his character and services. If his work in the circuit had heen productive of good he was very grateful to God: and if, as the addressistated, the people had been helped,comforted, and strengthened by his ministrations in public or in private he should cherish that assurance with great gratitude for the remainder of his life. The deepest and truest thanks of his heart went Out to all who had expressed in such hand- some terms their regard for him and appre- ciation of his services. During his connection with the Warrington circuit he had preached I'6oo times on circuit and at Penketh alone he had preached 326 sermons. On the list of sick whom he had visited were entered 121 names. Wherever the remant of his days might he spent he would cherish the remembrance of that night's gathering and their handsome testimonial as being amongst the most encouraging end helpful things of the whole course of his ministry. (Applause). It may be stated that the illuminated address has been beautifully executed byMr. R. Routledge,archi tect Penketh, and the text places on record the subscribers' " high appreciation of the recipient's character, and our sense of obligation to you for your ungrudging services rendered to the circuit during the fifteen years of your residence here." Testimony is borne to Mr. Gnnnell's conscientious devotion to duty, his uniform kindliness, approachableness, wise counsel, and broad and beautiful charity, and the address concludes by stating: "We shall hold the remembrance of you not only dear, but a sacred treasure." The signatures of Messrs. J. G.Within- ahaw and J. Upson (circuit stewards)and Mr. W. Silcock (circuit secretary) are appended.

To-day, League football has another chance of attracting.: patrons, though the top and bottom positions are practically settled. Warrington are at home to the gay and giddy Sall-rdians, and bearing in mind the walk-over which the Reds got last year owing to .Warrington's Cup-tie ambitions an 'effort will be made to lower the pride of the visitors. They seem to think they can always beat Warrington, and I hope the locals will teach them a much-needed lesson to-day.

A strong team has been selected by both clubs. Warrington are rejoicing in the fact that flockenhall will once more don the jersey after a long absence due to a fractured collar-bone. If the County man can reproduce his form of two months ago the Warrington team will be more formidable than ever. lam sorry that llockenhall's return causes Brooks' retirement. The" A " team emergency man has done splendid service in recent matches.

The Wai rington team will be: Hallam, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Dickinson, and Kenyon, three- . quarters ; Hockenhall and Davies, half-backs; Preston, Boardman, Thomas, Shugars, Belton, Swift, Jolley and Naylor, forwards.

1 The Salford team selected is as follows :—W. Lambert, full back ; R. M. H. Bell, W. Thomas, J. Lomas (capt.), and R. Richards, three-quarters ; D. Preston and W. D. John, half-backs ; J. Rhapps, D. Rees T. Lewis, J. Spencer, W. Brown', J. Williams,A. Buckler, S. Warwick, forwards. W. Hineshaw and G-. Baker, reserves.

On Monday the postponed visit to Widnes will be made. Kick-off at 6 p.m. Warrington will have plenty of opportunity this week-end of improving their record therefore. ,

The "A " team return to Combination football this week, and with the knowledge that Wigan "A ", lost a point against Chadderton on Saturday last, they will play to win at Salford. A strong team has been picked as follows :—Knight, full back ; Harris, Berry, J. Davies, and Jordan, three-quarters; R. Bate and Brooks, half backs ; Morrison, Coleman, Forster, Lunt, and Harmer, forwards.

Hockey. BY R.G.P. Stockton Heath played West Derby at Grappenhall- road last week, and this will be their last appearance on this ground, as the club have arranged to play on the \Varrington Cricket Ground next season. The cricket ground is one of the best enclosures in the North, and some of the county fixtures are sure to be played there next season.

The game against West Derby, was one of the most one-sided games plaYed at Stockton Heath this season, the score of three none in favour of the home side not representing the play in the least, as may be judged from the fact, that the home goalkeeper had not one shot to deal with during the whole game. The goals for Stockton Heath were scored by W. Banks one, W. Richardson two.

The season 1904-5 has been a fairly successful one,but the great blot against the club's record is the want of enthusiasm shown by the minor portion of the second eleven, although seeing that they were called upen to play eleven out of their last thirteen matches away fromEhome one must not be too hard. At the same time, to cancel eight matches and lose the remaining five during the second half of the season, shows that the club is in want of some new recruits. _ _ .

Cycling. , Acton Bridge should have been the destination of the Warrington club last Saturday, but the heavy rain in the early part of the afternoon made the prospects so discouraging that the rim was abandoned. Should the elements be favourable the club will journey to Lower Peover to-day.

Sankey Bridges Wesleyan C. C. open their season to- day with a run to quaint old Knntsford. Those of my readers who are members of this flourishing organ- ization will doubtless be pleased with the following attractive list of fixtures which has been arranged :

Is the cheap cycle doomed,? According to a rumour in Birmingham there is to be a trade combine to fight low prices. Host of the leading firms in the Midlands, it is stated, have for some time been complaining that there is no possibility of making a profit on the cheap machines, which are killing the trade in cycles of a better quality.

A grievance of motorists, and incidentally cyclists, in regard to newly metalled roads, was laid before the meeting of the Stockton Rural Council on Wednesday. A member drew attention to so many of the roads being in this condition, and suggested that half of the road should be done at a time so as to leave one side open. The surveyor said this was impossible if the roads were to be repaired evenly. The Council requested him to do the best he could to remedy the inconvenience com- plained of.

All over the country, it is stated, very large numbers of new clubs have been formed this year. From all accounts, the majority are in a very flourishing condition.

Bowling.

The Warrington Works Association have decided to act upon the suggestion made in a recent issue of the Examiner and to give the committee a greater freedom in the matter of re-arranging fixtures which cannot be fulfilled through unforeseen circumstances. The entries for the competition will close on May Ist and the draw will take place the same evening. Provided the weather is favourable play will commence the following Saturday.

The Bulls Head team intended thislyear making a bold bid for the championship of the Warrington and District League. Last year they were virtually favourites until the Cemetery club defeated them at home and away and so secured the lead. This year the Bulls Head players meant to avenge those defeats and last year's champions will have to be very much alive if they are to retain the honour. H. Fairbrother will lead them, as he hopes, on to a succession of victories, and in his abscence his place will be taken by A. Shaw, who has been elected sub-captain of the Bull's Head team.

The Liberal Club green will be opened for play to- morrow ueek and the opening main will be decided a week later.

It has been decided to confine the Warrington and District League to six clubs as last season there was some difficulty in arranging the fixtures. The club who retain membership are: Cemetery, Bull's Head, Horse and Jockey, Sankey Chapel House, Penketh Park, and the Farmers Arms, Fearnhead. Another depar- ture which has also been made from last season's pro- gramme should prove very acceptable to the members. It is the holding of a series of mains which have been arranged to be decided as ifollows : May 13th, on the Cemetery Green ; June 10th, Bull's Head ; July Bth, Fearnhead ; August 12th, Sankey ; and September 9th, Horse and Jockey. Home and away matches have also been arranged with the Widnes, Runcorn and District League to be played on the Bull's Head green on the 13th July and at Widnes on August 17th. Last year the Widnes League won on the two matches by 42 points and this season the Warrington League intend to reverse the result.

GREAT LIBERAL VICTORY AT BRIGHTON. Tory Majority of 3,165 Wiped Out. ' The Brighton election on Wednesday resulted in a most brilliant victory for the Liberal Party—the most tremendous, perhaps, looking to the character of the constituency in the whole series of by-elections of the past three years. The borough had not returned a Liberal previously since 1880, and the Conservatives, keeping the news of Mr. Gerald Loder' g appointment to a Junior Lordship of the Treasury private till the beginning of last week, sought to ensure that the Liberals should have no chance whatsoever. Yet the figures declared on Wednesday night were : Mr. E. A. Villiers (L) Mr. Gerald Loder (C) 7,392

Liberal majority Brighton has simply surpassed itself. in all the elections that have taken place there since 1885 inclu- sive the Conservative majority, has never fallen below 2,000, and n. recently as 1900 that majority was as high as 3,105. Now Mr. Villiers has swept the whole 3,165 aside and triumphantly won the seat by a majority of 817. .

The borough being a two-member one, it is difficult to mike a straight comparison with recent elections, but the following figures give the result of every contest there since 18.35: 1885. 1892. Sir W. Marriott (C)... 7,017 H. Loder (C) D. Smith (C) J. W. Probyn (L) J, R. Holland (L) 4,835

There are 20,439 electors on the register, and over 75 per cent. went to the poll on VVednesdiy.llow crushing is the result as against the Government may be gathered from even a cursory examination of the figures. Nevor before, for instance had anycandidate polled 8,000 votes in Brighton. Mr. Loder's own vote fell off by 4116 as compared with the last election, while the Liberal vote Increased by 3,516, surely a marvellous testimony to the way in which feeling against the Government is rising.

The Examiner, Saturday, April 8,1905.

PHYLLIS THE ELUSIVE.

Essence of Violets.

[By Godfrey Dean.]

"Is knowledge the greatest power?" Phyllis sked, with a pretty air of thought. To that, of course, there was only one answer. "No," I said,. "woman is." .

" Oh, I don't think so," she replied, meditatively. " Nor do I." She regarded me with surprise. "I don't think it, I know it." I explained. " But I don't see why you should. "For the best of all reasons." Perceiving rap purpose, Phyllis turned the subject slightly. " Tell me why she is, generally." A particular case—mine, of course—applies generally also. Therein lay justification for present- ing my personal views in the guise of general ones. "The reasons are innumerable," I said. "What are they?" "Are you prepared to hear a long statement of them ? "

"Yes, if you won't be so very serious," she said. "The reasons are grave." " I am waiting." "The reasons are," I said, "woman." She seemed disappointed, but I would not state it as a fact.

"Woman is not one reason, she is all the reasons. It is just as well you should know them, so I'll relate them."

"Why ought I to know them?" she asked. "That you may realise your power "—I fancy I saw a faint smile —"and the necessity of using it with discretion."

Phyllis leaned back in her chair with an air of resignation. But it is such a long story," I said," Perhaps—" "You might as well tell me, though," rather quickly. " One might think," I said, "everything exists for woman ; she is almost a religion—to man. She is certainly the great motive force that directs his mind and impels his actions. . . .

" Noi the desire for wealth ?" "For what purpose ?" I asked. Phyllis mistook effect for cause.

"Woman is a perfect armoury. Her chief weapons are smiles, cajolery, tears. If she fails to influence a man with the first, she will weaken his will with the second, and tears complete his destruction." "I don't think it is so," she said. " Alen don't like to see tears."

"No, that is why women use them. If a woman doesn't, it is because there is no necessity." "Why should you know ?" she asked. It was a mistake, but I gave her no clkante to correct it. "For the best of all reason," I said—watching the increase of colour in her face—" because of you." "You must be quite impersonal," she said. "You tempted me," I said. Adam was the first of a long procession. "Indeed, I didn't," indignant in denial. "I mean, unconsciously you did. After all," I said, daringly, but pleadingly, "that was my only answer. I should not have known of it but for you." I caught the expression of interest in her eyes, therefore I continued.

"You cannot deny it, can you ?" "I can't if I wished (giving me a swift look of interrogation) because I don't know." "My authority is unimpeachable." "And who is your authority?" bending her head. Was it only to inhale the fragrance of the violets at her breast? "Myself, the highest." , " Really ; but you're an interested person." Then quickly, "I mean---" "Very," I said, unscrupulously assisting' in her embarrassment. (Curiously, considering circum- stances, Phyllis is always sensitive in this manner.) "Oh, I meant," she said, "that--" She realised that explanation was hopeless, so I came to her rescue.

"Don't," I begged. "Why should you? I would rather it remained unexplained; ta put my own con- struction on it."

"ft might not be mine," she said. "Not entirely ; we should shire the opinion, I hope." I think Phyllis despaired of me, so revertod to the original point. " How can you say you would never have known but for me ?"

At least, in avoiding an answer she accepted what I wished. "I could not have been interested—or so much—in other women," I said. " Assertion isn't argumont,"deinurely. Phyllis has a trick, occasionally, of confronting me with my own phrases. " It is an assertion of a fact." "Perhaps you are intsrested in other women, though ?" " I am," I said. "How many ?" very quietly: " All women. And a woman, even a really charm- ing woman—which is saying much—never does know the extent of interest she has for men."

" Oh ?" very soft and very low, and onca again she bent her head over her bouquet—mine. Then she unpinned it from her bosom. " It has lost its perfume," she said, carelessly, and laying it down. I appeared unconscious of the significance of this incident. _ _ _ .

" She is a world of interest. Her actions are events to be noted, criticised, and to draw conclusions from; the smallest is suggestive to the man who is open to be interested,and he is either a lover or a misogynist." " Why the misogynist ?" she asked, as if more in- terested in him. "Because he is one. The misogynist only arrives at his state of mind through love—or lack of it Cupid having failed to pierce two particular hearts with one arrow." "And the other ?" apparently indifferently, and as an after thought. " The lover's interest is quite different," I said. "He is constantly comparing other women with the one he loves. And always with the same result. I How more delightful she is, with charms others do not possess, or he cannot see ! But being a lover, he must study the inspiration of love." I " For instance, I can recall your characteristic actions, The turn and poise of your head,"—l stopped. Phyllis had picked up the violets and hidden her face in them. " I was mistaken," she said, putting them in her dress again—" wasn't I?" ! I could detect the perfume at a distance ; and, yes, I much more close to. They were quite fresh. But I don't think she was mistaken.

PUNK ETH STUDENTS SOCIAL.—A very enjoyable gathering was held on Friday last in the Wesleyan School, when the students attending the Evening Con- Urination classes, met, to close the Session. The gathering is an annual one, promoted by the teachers, and is confined exclusively to the members attending the classes. COMMERCIAL TRAVELLER AND HIS

COMMISSION.

Warrington Man's Successful Action.

His Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C., on Thurs- day decided a point which is of considerable interest to commercial travellers. The point was raised in a counter-claim to the action brought by John Smith wholesale furniture manufacturer, Red Bank, Man- chester. to recover from Heary Ellis Vane, 9, Liverpool- road, Warrington, auctioneer and commission agent, the sum of £5 133. Bd., collected on behalf of the plaintiff by the defendant. Mr. Vane counterclaimed the slim of £2l 18s. as commission noon orders obtained by him. ' In his counter-claim Mr. Vane credited Smith with £8 is. 3c1., leaving a net amen it of £l3 17s. Gd., which he sought to recover. Mr. Newman, Manchester represented, Mr. Smith I Mr. Vane being represented by Mr. A. Browne. The claim was not disputed and Mr. Browne at once ' proceeded with the counter-claim. He explained that his client in 1902 entered into an agreement with the plaintiff to sell his goods on 5 per cent commission. ' Mr. Vane in a letter to Mr. Smith agreed to the amount of the commission provided that it was paid upon all repeat orders. At the first settling between the parties ' commission was pPid upon repeat orders. Mr. Newman: It is upon orders not got by Mr. Vane that we refuse to pay. If a man deals direct with us we say we are not liable. Mr. Browne continuing, argued that his client, or any commercial traveller who obtained a new customer, was entitled to commission upon all business done by that customer whether canvassed personally or sent direct to the firm. Mr. Vane secured a large number of orders for Mr. Smith and had also found that several of the customers which he introduced had sent repeat orders. On September 23th,1903, he discovered that under 'Mr. Smith's orders another man was travelling his customers and underselling him. He wrote to Mr. Smith com- plaining about this but received no reply. Mr. Wane then pressed for a settlement, in response to which Mr. Smith credited him with some £2 commission for two year's work. Mr. Vane then found that he had only been credited with commission upon orders which he had sent direct to the firm, whereas he contended he was entitled to commission upon all repeat orders. Upon resuming after lunch his Honour said he had hoped that some course would have been taken which would have dispensed with the necessity of his trying that action. Mr. Newman and Mr. Browne had a brief consultation as the result of which Mr. Browne said the parties had agreed to terms. What they agreed was that judgment should be given for Vane on the claim, without costs and for £8 Bs. upon the counter claim with costs on scale "A." His Honour said the question appeared to him to be one of degree and was very much influenced by time. If a man introduced a customer and secured from him an order for £1 and then within reasonable time, the agent not being discharged in the meantime and the original relations existing, it certainly did seem unreasonable and contrary to all business principles that' the commission of the agent should be limited to the amount of the original trans- action. At the same time he could easily conceive that there might be circumstances in which the length of time which elapsed would render it unreasonable to assume that there should be a continuance of the original agreement which would entitle the agent to commission upon repeat orders. In that case he thought Mr. New- man had acted wisely and that the course which had been adopted was reasonable and proper.

Mrs. WILKINSON submitted the following resolution : —" That this Board expresses itself in favour of the Bill to be introduced to the House of Commons by Mr. Charles Schwann, M.P., to prevent the Disfranchise- ment of "persons receiving Poor Law Relief " and that copies of this resolution be forwarded to the Prime Minister ; the President of the Local Govern- ment Board ; Mr. Robert Pierpoint M.P. ; and Colonel Pilkington, M.P." She said she would like the Board to use its influence in favour of the Voting Disqualfica- tion Removal Bill that would be before Parliament shortly, namely "that no person shall be deprived of any right to any franchise by reason only that he or any member of his family has received out-relief." It was their duty as Guardians to do their utmost to remoie any obstacle that might stand between the poor and their rights, and so long as a person held a voting quali- fication she did not think it was right to take his vote from him, She failed to understand why people should forfeit their civic rigilts simply be3ause they were poor. Mr. SETON seconded. Mr. FORSIIANY : Does the resolution include the inmates of the House ? The CKAIRMA.N : If the inmates of the House have votes I suppose it includes them. They were supposed to be trying to work for purity in public life all round, but if the Bill in question became law it would really be setting the hands of the clock back and making it possible for candidates to bribe the electors out of the rates. It would be a most lamentable thing if that Board or any public body countenanced anything of that kind directly , or indirectly, :and he should vote against the resolution. ' The Ray. Da. WHAT THE RAGGED SCHOOL MISSION IS DOING. _— Brealdastless School Children.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Poor Law Relief and Disfranchisement.

COUNTY COUNCIL EXPENDITURE CRITICISED.

The final meeting of this Board for the year 1901-5 was held at the Board Room on Wednesday morning, Mr. A. Brooks presiding. There were also present : Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. Dr. Mercer, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, R. Forshavv, W. Hickman, J. Billings, G. Thompson, S. Davies, J. Payton W. Houghton, R. Bolton, W. Flannagan, M. W. geton, T. H. Ashcroft, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (super- tenclent of Padgate schools). _ VA CC DZA.TION FEES.

When the list of cheques came up for sanction, Mr. S. DA.VIES said he would be glad when the vaccination fees ceased. The CLERK' : There is a new order issued, but at present it is in the hands of the Government. PADG (TE SCHOOLS.

At a meeting of the Padgate Schools Committee votes of thanks were accorded to the Chairman (Mr. G. Mather) and Mrs. Clay, who occupies the vice-chair, for their services during the past year. SLEEPERS WANTED.

The Board conarmed the Lions° Committee's decision to invite tenders for the supply of sleepers from the railway companies. NURSE'S NEW APPOINTMENT. Nurse Simmons notified the Board that she had obtained the post of head norse at Northampton Union indrmary and asked to be released from her duties at Warrington in a month. The necessary permission was granted and it was decided to advertise for a successor. LUNACY CONIYIISSIONERS' REPORT. OVERCROWDING IN THE HOSPITAL. NEW PADDED 1200 M NEEDED.

Dr. Coog.P., visitor on behalf of the Lunacy Commis- I sioners, reported on his visit to the House on February 21th. There were in the Workhouse classed as imbeciles I and permanently detained on orders produced to him I thirty inmates of each sex. The majority of them had been placed on the list since the last visit of the Com- missioner. He also saw a man who had been recently admitted upon A fourteen days order. • They were all , proper cases for detention, but no one appeared unfit ,tobe in the Workhouse. More than a fourth of the total number were confined to bed—an unusually large proportion. Hardly any of these were seriously bodily ill, but some were very feeble. He had no complaint and was quite satisfied with the personal conditions of the patients and with the state of their clothing and bedding. Of the men, twenty-three were warded in the lunatic block, which was for men only, four in the body of the House, and four in the hospital; whilst of the women, twenty three were in the hospital and seven in the house. The accommodation thus pro- vided for them was good, the lunatic block -having by re-decoration and the provision of new furniture, another lavatory, and an alternative exit from the dormitories, been greatly improved since Mr. Urmson's visit. Some overcrowding was, however, notice- able in the hospital. A well-equipped padded- room attacheA to the lunatic block was much needed. Both the existing ones were Po badly dilapidated and badly placed in the old Infirmary that they could not either with advantage or safety be used. The conse- quence was that the return of mechanical restraint by the jacket was unduly high, as many as thirty-six patients having been subjected, some for a good many hours, to this method of treatment, which it was desirable to keep within the narrowest !imits. He understood that for the imbeciles there were now two attendants, one for day one one for night, of each sex, who were permanently on the staff, but that all of them had contracted out of their right to a pension. Mr. HICKAIAN remarked that the report appeared to be satisfactory. The CLERK : Yes. There is no complaint. The consideration of the Commissioners' recommend- ations was referred to the House Committee. Mr. EDWARD J. DIGNUNI wrote expressing, on behalf of himself and members of the family, their thanks and appreciation of the kindly reference to their late father made by sevnral members of the Board, and of the vote of condolence extended to- them in their great and irreparable loss.

OVERSEER FOR NEWTON. Tt was reported that Messrs. E. L. Bent, J. Sankey, John Simpson, and Dr. J. H. Patterson had been ap- pointed overseers of the poor for the township of Newton for the ensuing year. POOR LW RELIEF AND DISFRANCHISEMENT. MRS. WILKINSON'S MOTION.

On a vote being taken the amendment to defer they matter was carried.

CRITICISM OF COUNTY EXPENDITURE. "WRETCHED MANAGEMENT" OF EDUCATIONAL AFFAIRS-

_ The Clock submitted precepts prepared in accordance with the app oved estimates. The calls fixed at the rate of sevenpenco in the £ on assessable values( hence less than if calculated on rateable values) were as follows—Burton- wood, £528; Cuerdley, £166; Haydock, £852; Houghton, £9O; Newton, £2,187 (inclusive of £63 separate charges); Penketh, £256 ; Poulton, £lB7 : Rixton, £213 ; Great Sankey, £268 ; Southworth, £lOl ; Winwick, £629 : Woolson, £lO9 ; Warrington, £7,423 (inclusive of £4O separate charges). The calls were divided in two parts payable on May 10th and July sth. In the year 1864, the two Lancashire County contributions, inclusive of Warrington, totalled £1,816. At much more recent periods they totalled under £3,000. In 1903-4, without inclusion of the Borough, hence upon a rateable value less by more than one half of the total Union rateable value the two demands totalled £10,497 3s. id. and in 1904-5 they totalled £14,685 4s. sd. Although upon the recent revision the County rateable value greatly increased, the Overseers of Townships might expect further serious additions to the County demands. It would be imprudent if they reduced their poor rate in the £ because of reductions now being made in Union and Rural District contributions.

Mr. THOMPSON asked if Mr. Sutton could tell the Board what the serious addition to the County demands was going to be. As an overseer, he wanted to know, as in these bad times he was anxious to give the rate- payers the full benefit of any reduction they could possibly allow. The CLERK said that in rural parishes one penny was being taken off the Union rate and one penny off the District Council rate, but he advised overseers not to take the whole twopence off their next rate, because he, was quite sure that the County Council would put at least one penny—and they might put twopence—on their rate. It would bo an awkward position for the over- seers to be called upon to levy that additional rate— chiefly for education—unless they provided for it. The only way of meeting the demand in that case would be to levy a separate rate. He could practically tell them that a penny would be put on; it might be twopence or even more.

Mr. THOMPSON said the Board of Guardians and Rural District Council had tried to keep down expenses and it was time the County Council did soland tried to help the ratepayers in some kway. The education ex- penses of the County were very heavy, and there was no doubt the County Council Education Committee were in a fog. He did not think they knew what they were doing. He spoke as a school manager, and his view was that the wretched way in which things were managed was a disgrace to the County. Mr. HICKMAN : Will you move a resolution on it? Mr. THOMPSON : Yes. I move that the County Council be requested to curtail expenses in all ways they can. Mr. HlcicmAN seconded.

The CLERK thought it would be advisable to wait and see what the rates amounted to before passing such a resolution. _

Mr. BrraJNGE did not think the overseers could expect any reduction of rates in view of the excessive call which would come from the County Council. It would not be safe to think of reducing their call until the County Council or Education Committee knew exactly where they were, and got out of the seeming t The CHAIRMAN said the question was not one which affected the Warrington Borough Guardians and he sug- gested that it would be better for Mr. Thompson to move the resolution at the District Council meeting. Mr. THOMPSON : It would perhaps be as well to leave the resolution alone. The matter then dropped. THE CHAIRMAN THANKED. _ _ COMPLIMENTARY SPEECHES.

Mr. S. DAVIES said they were reminded that day that the year's work was ended, and he therefore rose to perform the agreeable duty of moving a vote of thanks to the Chairman fur his services during the past year. Mr. Brooks had done the work most ably. and impartially. He (Mr. Davies) had at times heard it said that the chairman had been partial and one-sided in some things, but personally he failed to remember when that had been so. If Mr. Brooks had happened to err in that respect he the Chairman) was sure he had done so unintentionally. His decisions on points of order had been admirable.

Mr. Fousnaw seconded and remarked that be joined the Board in the same year as Mr. Brooks, and he had always admired the chairman for his level-headedness and his conscientious methods. He had done much to dignify the chair and to uphold its traditions. Mr. ASHCROFT supported and said Mr. Brooks had been most devoted to his duties as chairman of the Board and of the House Committee, and could be well described as a good and faithful servant. Mr. HICKMAN remarked that he had had occasion to differ with the Chairman, but he hoped he was large' hearted enough to forgive all differences of opinion when Mr. Brooks had finished his work and to support the vote of thanks most cordially. The Rev. Dr. MEncErt esteemed it a privilege to support the vote of thanks. During the twelve months he had been a member of the Board he had often had the opportunity of admiring Mr. Brooks's conduct in the chair. Ele had admired his wonderful cheerfulness, when the Guar- dians had tried his patience very much—(laughter)--- and his great urbanity coupled with great firmness and business-like decision. If Mr. Brooks could act as chair- man for a considerable time longer he (Dr. Mercer) for one would be delighted. Mr. IVIATHER also endorsed all that had been said. Mr. JOLLEY said that as one who had been associated with Mr. Brooks ever since he became a member of the Board on various committees and sub-committees, and had seen something of the whole-hearted devotion to the interests of the Union which the Chairman had always displayed, he could not let the opportunity pass without adcFng a tribute to his merits. Mr. Brooks had a very firm grasp of the principles which underlay the Poor Law, and he had acquired 5 mastery of the details of administration which was exceptional and which had materially helped hint in carrying out the duties in such an admirable manner. He had won the esteem and respect of all the members whether they had agreed or disagreed with his views orl• particular subjects. Miss BROA.DI3ENT supported and said she had found it very pleasant to work under Mr. Brooks's chairmanship. At first she did not want to. be a Guardian at all,but she' thought there ought to be another lady Guardian. Instead of disliking the meetings, as she expected sha would, she had thoroughly enjoyed them. Complimentary speeches were also delivered 11 Messrs. Billing°, Payton and Flannagan, and the motion was heartily agreed to. In responding, the CHAritmAN said he thought there' must be something in the atmosphere that morning which gave them a rosy view of everything. (Laughter.) Under the circumstances, however, he was very pleased that that kindly feeling existed They had had a° electrical atmosphere for some little time, but the nega- tive pole seemed to agree with the positive pole that morning. There had never been ranch thunder and now' they had got into a quiescent state and found that beneath the elecrical storms there was a solid founda; tion of trust and confidence in each other. He ha'', always had confidence in every member of the Boara trying to do his best and although he had perhaps et across occasionally with some of them, it had heew merely a matter of the moment. He hoped his successor in the chair would receive the same kindly consideratio° they had always extended to him (Mr. Brooks). A vote of thanks was accorded to the vice-chairma`: (Mr. J. W. Knowles) on the motion of Mr. lirciot' secondei by Mr. ASHCROFT.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. Further Protest Against the Expenditure. • ---- , The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural , District Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday when ' there were present: Messrs. S. Woodward (presiding), C. Roylance, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, J. Mounfield, Egerton Warburton, J. Stokes, J. Whitlow, A. Broome I T. Baxter, H. M. Davies, C. E. Linaker, J. H. Davies, J. Smith, T. Wright. H. Mawdsley, T. Guest, W. Harrison, S. Horton, J. L. Jones, E. Bretherton, C. Horton, R. A. Chrimes, J. Fryer, Spruce, W. M. Morris, J. Rutter, J. Hallows, J. Whitley, R. Billington, C. E. Linaker, j jun., J. Mounfleld, A. J. Guest, G. P. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), Swinton and Diggle (Sur- veyors), J. Farrington (inspector), and Dr. Adams (medical officer). WATER SUPPLY AT PRESTON-WTI-I-HILL. Mr. James Whitlow reported that at the annual Parish meeting for,Preston-olh-Hill, a resolution was passed lh asking the District Council to take steps to compel the Manchester Ship Canal Company, the London and North-Western Railway Company, and the North Stafford Canal Company, to have the water supply con- nected to cottages otvned by them in the township. I Mr. Ashton said he had written to the three firms ' named. He did not think there would be any difficulty as the firms seemed willing to do what was required of them. THE INCREASE IN THE RATES. The clerk read a resolution adopted at the annual parish meeting for Stockton Heath calling upon the Council and the County Council to exercise a ' greater economy in the matter of expenses. I He also read the following resolution adopted by the ' Parish Council of Frodsham :—" That this meeting views with alarm the continued increase in the rates,and more especially lathe calls for the County Council, and i ask that n the interests of the ratepayers a check should be put on extravagance." No comment was made upon either resolution. ALTERATIONS IN CHURCH-STREET, FRODSHAAI. The Chairman reported that the committee appointed , visited the site of Mr. Jones' property, Church-street, j Frodsham. They thought that if the owners of the lower property were prepared to carry out the suggestion that they should contribute £lO the District Council would be recommended to erect a wall the whole length. In , the event of the property owners not agreeing to that , suggestion. The Council would be recommended to accept the offer of Mr. Jones and allow him to enclose I whatever land was necessary in order to straighten his line of frontage. DRAINAGE AT LATCHFORD. Mr. George Pilling wrote stating that the Latchford Parish Council had passed a resolution asking the District Council to take immediate steps to alter and improve the drainage of Latchford Without. The Clerk was instructed to write to Mr. Pilling for particulars with regard to the request of the Latchford Parish Council. I •
